The first and most common wildcard is the asterisk (*). It matches any sequence of characters, including an empty string. For example, b* matches any filename starting with the letter "b" (such as b, ba, bab, or bcdb).

Slightly less common is the question mark (?). It matches exactly one instance of any single character. Thus, b? matches ba and bb, but will not match b or bab.

darkstar:~$ touch b ba bab 
darkstar:~$ ls * 
b ba bab 
darkstar:~$ ls b? 
ba

The fun doesn't stop there! bash also supports square brackets ([ ]), which allow you to specify exact sets or ranges of characters to match at a single position. Unlike shell brace expansion, characters inside brackets are not separated by commas.

You can list individual characters inside the brackets (e.g., [ab]) or specify ranges of letters and numbers using a hyphen (e.g., [a-z] or [0-9]):

darkstar:~$ touch a1 a2 a3 a4 a5 a6 a7 a8 a9 10
darkstar:~$ ls a[1-4,9] 
a1 a2 a3 a4 a9

Because Linux and ASCII collation are case-sensitive, uppercase and lowercase letters are treated as distinct characters. In standard ASCII ordering, all uppercase letters (A–Z) come before lowercase letters (a–z). Therefore, when specifying ranges that span both uppercase and lowercase letters, you must list the character with the lower ASCII value first.

For example, [W-b] is a valid range (matching W, X, Y, Z, a, and b), whereas [w-B] is invalid because w comes after B in ASCII order:

darkstar:~$ ls 1[W-b] 
1W 1X 1Y 1Z 1a 1b 
darkstar:~$ ls 1[w-B] 
/bin/ls: cannot access 1[b-W]: No such file or directory

In the second example, because [w-B] is an invalid range (since w has a higher ASCII value than B), bash fails to expand the wildcard pattern and treats 1[w-B] as a literal filename. Since no file with that exact name exists, ls returns an error.

5.5 Input and Output Redirection

One of the defining features of Linux and UNIX-like operating systems is the availability of many small, specialized applications and the ability to chain them together to build complex systems. This modularity is achieved by redirecting the output of one program to a file or another program, or by drawing input from an external file or stream.

By default, every command in bash interacts with three standard data channels known as file descriptors: * 0 (stdin) — Standard Input (keyboard input by default) * 1 (stdout) — Standard Output (terminal display by default) * 2 (stderr) — Standard Error (error messages displayed on screen)

Redirecting Standard Output (> and >>)

darkstar:~$ echo foo 
foo 
darkstar:~$ echo foo > /tmp/bar 
darkstar:~$ cat /tmp/bar 
foo

If you want to preserve existing content and append new lines to the end of the file instead, use the >> operator:

darkstar:~$ echo foo2 >> /tmp/bar 
darkstar:~$ cat /tmp/bar 
foo 
foo2

Redirecting Standard Error (2>)

Error messages are sent to standard error (stderr) rather than standard output (stdout). To redirect error messages to a file, specify file descriptor 2 before the redirection symbol (2>):

darkstar:~$ rm bar 
rm: cannot remove 'bar': No such file or directory 

darkstar:~$ rm bar 2> /tmp/err.log 
darkstar:~$ cat /tmp/err.log 
rm: cannot remove 'bar': No such file or directory

Note: Since stdout is descriptor 1, 1> is functionally identical to >. You can also redirect both stdout and stderr into the same file using &> (or 2>&1).

Redirecting Standard Input (<)

You can instruct a program to read its input directly from a file instead of waiting for keyboard input using the < operator:

darkstar:~$ fromdos < dosfile.txt

Pipelines (|)

Perhaps the most powerful feature of bash is the pipe operator (|). A pipeline connects the standard output of one command directly into the standard input of another, enabling you to combine utilities seamlessly:

darkstar:~$ ps auxw | grep getty
root      1500  0.0  0.0   5616  3288 tty1     Ss+  out27   0:00 /sbin/agetty --noclear 38400 tty1 linux
root      1501  0.0  0.0   5616  3308 tty2     Ss+  out27   0:00 /sbin/agetty 38400 tty2 linux
root      1502  0.0  0.0   5616  3308 tty3     Ss+  out27   0:00 /sbin/agetty 38400 tty3 linux
root      1503  0.0  0.0   5616  3380 tty4     Ss+  out27   0:00 /sbin/agetty 38400 tty4 linux
root      1504  0.0  0.0   5616  3312 tty5     Ss+  out27   0:00 /sbin/agetty 38400 tty5 linux
root      1505  0.0  0.0   5616  3464 tty6     Ss+  out27   0:00 /sbin/agetty 38400 tty6 linux

In this example, ps aux lists all running processes on the system, and the pipe passes that text output directly to grep getty, which filters and displays only the lines matching "getty".

5.6 Task Management

bash provides robust task management features, allowing you to suspend, resume, and manage multiple running programs from a single terminal session. This enables you to temporarily pause a process, return to the shell prompt to perform other work, and resume the paused task later—either in the foreground or in the background.

Suspending Tasks (Ctrl+Z and jobs)

Pressing Ctrl+Z sends a signal to the active process, pausing it and returning you to the shell prompt. You can suspend multiple applications indefinitely.

To view all currently suspended or backgrounded tasks, use the jobs built-in command:

darkstar:~$ jobs 
[1]- Stopped vi TODO 
[2]+ Stopped vi chapter_05.xml

In the output above: - The number in brackets ([1], [2]) represents the Job ID. - The plus sign (+) indicates the current (most recently suspended) job. - The minus sign (-) indicates the previous job.

Foregrounding Tasks (fg)

To resume a suspended process in the foreground, use the fg command. Running fg without any arguments resumes the current job (marked with +). To bring a specific job back, pass its Job ID (e.g., fg 1 or fg %1):

darkstar:~$ fg 
# Resumes job [2] ("vi chapter_05.xml")

darkstar:~$ fg 1 
# Resumes job [1] ("vi TODO")

Backgrounding Tasks (bg and &)

If you want a suspended command to continue executing in the background without taking over your terminal prompt (useful for long-running scripts, downloads, or compilations), use the bg command:

darkstar:~\$ bg 1
[1]+ vi TODO &

Tip: You can also launch a command directly in the background from the start by appending an ampersand (&) to the end of the command line (e.g., command &).

5.7 Terminals

Slackware Linux and other UNIX-like operating systems allow users to interact with them in many ways, but the most common—and arguably the most useful—is the terminal. In the past, terminals were physical keyboards and monitors connected to a mainframe or server via serial cables. Today, however, most terminals are virtual; they exist purely in software, eliminating the need for expensive dedicated hardware.

The most basic virtual terminals in Slackware are managed by agetty. By default, Slackware runs six virtual console instances (tty1 through tty6), allowing local users to log in and run commands simultaneously. You can switch between these text-based consoles using the Alt+F1 through F6 key combinations (or Ctrl+Alt+F1–F6 if you are currently inside a graphical session). This multi-console capability is particularly useful on headless servers or for running separate tasks under different user accounts.

On desktop and laptop workstations where a graphical interface (X) is running, users typically rely on graphical terminal emulators. Slackware includes several options, such as KDE's konsole, XFCE's Terminal, and the classic xterm. These can usually be launched from your desktop environment's menu (often listed under a "System" or "Utilities" submenu). Opening any of these applications creates a new terminal window that automatically starts your default shell (bash).

5.8 Customization

By now, you should be fairly familiar with bash, and you may have even noticed some seemingly odd behavior. For example, when you log in at a text console, you are presented with a prompt that looks like this:

brusso@darkstar:~$

However, inside certain terminal windows, you might see a much less informative prompt:

bash-3.1$

This variation occurs because different types of shell sessions read different configuration files upon startup:

  • Login Shells: Executed when you log into the system (e.g., at the console prompt or via SSH). A login shell reads /etc/profile first for global settings, followed by the user's ~/.bash_profile.
  • Interactive Non-Login Shells: Launched when you open a terminal window inside a graphical desktop environment. An interactive shell reads ~/.bashrc instead.

This distinction allows power users to fine-tune environment behavior, but it can confuse new users who want a consistent environment everywhere. To unify your settings, you can edit your ~/.bashrc file to automatically source your login profile:

#~/.bashrc 
. /etc/profile 
. ~/.bash_profile

With this configuration in place, both login and interactive shells will share identical settings. From then on, you only need to edit ~/.bash_profile for personal shell customizations and /etc/profile for system-wide changes.

Configuring the Prompt (PS1)

The appearance of your bash prompt is controlled by the PS1 environment variable. By default, Slackware defines PS1 as follows:

darkstar:~$ echo $PS1 
\u@\h:\w\$

Every literal character in PS1 is displayed as-is, while characters preceded by a backslash (\) are interpreted as special escape sequences:

  • \u — The current username
  • \h — The hostname up to the first dot
  • \H — The full hostname
  • \w — The current working directory (with $HOME abbreviated as ~)
  • \$ — Displays # if the current user is root, or $ for a standard user
  • \d — The current date (e.g., "Wed Jan 14")
  • \@ — The current time in 12-hour AM/PM format
  • \n — Inserts a newline
  • \j — The number of background jobs currently managed by the shell
  • \l — The basename of the shell's terminal device (e.g., 1 for tty1 or pts/0)

Here are two examples of custom multi-line prompts and the PS1 values used to create them:

brusso@darkstar:~$ echo $PS1 
\u@\h:\w\$

For comprehensive guidance on creating advanced prompt layouts—including adding ANSI color codes—refer to the documentation in /usr/doc/Linux-HOWTOs/Bash-Prompt-HOWTO.

5.9 Another words about Shell

The shell is far more than a simple command-line interface—it is an indispensable Swiss Army knife for system administration. Throughout this chapter, we have seen how features like environment variables, wildcards, I/O redirection, pipelines, and job control transform routine terminal operations into powerful, streamlined workflows.

Beyond executing standalone commands, the shell enables you to automate complex tasks, build customized system maintenance scripts, and manage your Slackware environment with total control. Mastering bash is one of the most rewarding investments you can make in your Linux journey. We strongly encourage you to keep exploring, writing your own shell scripts, and discovering new ways to elevate your productivity.

6. Process Control

Slackware systems often run hundreds or thousands of programs, each of which is referred to as a process in a Linux environment. Managing these processes is an important part of system administration.

So how exactly do we handle all of these separate processes? To help us with this task, there are several utilities we can use. In this chapter, we will explore some of the most common ones.

6.1 ps

The first step in managing processes is figuring out what processes are currently running. The most popular and powerful tool for this is ps. Without any arguments, ps won't tell you much information. By default, it only tells you what processes are running in your currently active shell. If we want more information, we'll need to look deeper.


darkstar:~$ ps 
  PID TTY           TIME CMD 
12220 pts/4     00:00:00 bash 
12236 pts/4     00:00:00 ps

Here you can see what processes you are running in your currently active shell or terminal and only some information is included. The PID is the "Process ID"; every process is assigned a unique number. The TTY tells you what terminal device the process is attached to. Naturally, CMD is the command that was run. You might be a little confused by TIME though, since it seems to move so slowly. This isn't the amount of real time the process has been running, but rather the amount of CPU time the process has consumed. An idle process uses virtually no CPU time, so this value may not increase quickly.

Here, you can see what processes you are running in your currently active shell or terminal, with only basic information included. The PID is the "Process ID" — every process is assigned a unique number. The TTY tells you what terminal device the process is attached to. Naturally, CMD is the command that was run. You might be a little confused by TIME, though, since it seems to move so slowly. This isn't the amount of real time the process has been running, but rather the amount of CPU time the process has consumed. An idle process uses virtually no CPU time, so this value may not increase quickly.

Viewing only our own processes isn't very much fun, so let's take a look at all the processes on the system with the -e argument.


darkstar:~$ ps -e 
PID TTY         TIME     CMD 
  1 ?        00:00:00 init 
  2 ?        00:00:00 kthreadd 
  3 ?        00:00:00 pool_workqueue_release
  4 ?        00:00:00 kworker/R-rcu_gp
  5 ?        00:00:00 kworker/R-sync_wq
  6 ?        00:00:00 kworker/R-kvfree_rcu_reclaim
  7 ?        00:00:00 kworker/R-slub_flushwq
  8 ?        00:00:00 kworker/R-netns
 10 ?        00:00:00 kworker/0:0H-kblockd
 11 ?        00:00:00 kworker/0:1-events
 12 ?        00:00:01 kworker/u64:0-gfx
 13 ?        00:00:00 kworker/R-mm_percpu_wq
 14 ?        00:00:00 ksoftirqd/0
 15 ?        00:00:01 rcu_preempt
 16 ?        00:00:00 rcu_exp_par_gp_kthread_worker/0
 17 ?        00:00:00 rcu_exp_gp_kthread_worker
 18 ?        00:00:00 migration/0
 19 ?        00:00:00 kprobe-optimizer
... many more lines omitted ...

Note

The above example uses the standard ps syntax, but much more information can be discovered if we use BSD syntax. In order to do so, we must use the aux argument.

This is distinct from the -aux argument, but in most cases the two arguments are equivalent. This is a decades-old relic. For more information, see the man page for ps.


darkstar:~$ ps aux 
USER    PID     %CPU    %MEM    VSZ     RSS TTY STAT START TIME COMMAND 
root      1     0.0     0.0     3928    632 ?   Ss   15:45 0:00 init [4] 
root      2     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   S    15:45 0:00 [kthreadd]
root      3     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   S    15:45 0:00 [pool_workqueue_release]
root      4     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   I<   15:45 0:00 [kworker/R-rcu_gp]
root      5     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   I<   15:45 0:00 [kworker/R-sync_wq]
root      6     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   I<   15:45 0:00 [kworker/R-kvfree_rcu_reclaim]
root      7     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   I<   15:45 0:00 [kworker/R-slub_flushwq]
root      8     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   I<   15:45 0:00 [kworker/R-netns]
root     10     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   I<   15:45 0:00 [kworker/0:0H-kblockd]
root     11     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   I    15:45 0:00 [kworker/0:1-events]
root     12     0.1     0.0        0      0 ?   I    15:45 0:01 [kworker/u64:0-gfx]
root     13     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   I<   15:45 0:00 [kworker/R-mm_percpu_wq]
root     14     0.0     0.0        0      0 ?   S    15:45 0:00 [ksoftirqd/0]
root     15     0.1     0.0        0      0 ?   I    15:45 0:01 [rcu_preempt]
... many more lines omitted ....

As you can see, the aux argument offers much more information, including which user controls the process and what percentage of CPU and RAM the process is consuming when ps is run.

To accomplish this on a per-process basis, ps allows one or more process IDs (PIDs) to be provided on the command line and features the -o flag to display specific attributes of a PID.

darkstar:~$ ps -o cmd -o etime
CMD                             ELAPSED
/bin/bash                         09:21
fish                              09:21
ps -o cmd -o etime                00:00

What this is displaying, is the PID's command name (cmd), and its elapsed time (etime). The PID in this example, is a shell variable for the PID of the current shell. So you can see, in this example, the shell process has existed for 9 minutes, 21 seconds.

By combining ps with the pgrep command, this process can be easily automated:

darkstar:~$  ps -o cmd -o rss -o vsz $(pgrep chrome)
CMD                           RSS    VSZ
/opt/google/chrome/chrome   516976 51688644
/opt/google/chrome/chrome_c  4396 50355400
/opt/google/chrome/chrome_c  3812 50346164
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 65816 50774672
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 66164 50774660
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 19420 50774688
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 196028 51243208
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 153324 50886676
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 57752 50858720
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 158208 1518473728
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 353032 1524425320
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 512404 1518545988
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 289524 1518470868
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 94932 50848384
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 122228 1518466812
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 165912 1518475280
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 198144 1518534900
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 53908 67618392
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 418120 1518642108
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 112568 1518360188
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 126632 1520336712
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 136956 1518380384
/opt/google/chrome/chrome - 77984 1518350972

In this example, command substitution using $(pgrep chrome) returns the PIDs of all running processes whose command names match 'chrome'. ps then displays each process's command name (cmd), resident set size (rss), and virtual memory size (vsz).

Finally, ps can also create a process tree. This shows you which processes have children processes. Ending the parent of a child process also ends the child. We do this with the -ejH argument.


darkstar:~$ ps -ejH 
... many lines omitted ... 
3091  3091  3091 ?        00:00:00   login
3111  3111  3111 tty2     00:00:00     bash
3130  3130  3111 tty2     00:00:00       sddm.bin
3135  3135  3135 tty7     00:00:00         Xorg

3176  3130  3111 tty2     00:00:01           sddm-greeter-qt
3182  3130  3111 tty2     00:00:00   dbus-launch

3327  3239  3239 ?        00:00:00     sddm-helper
3343  3239  3239 ?        00:00:00       dbus-run-sessio
3365  3239  3239 ?        00:00:01         dbus-daemon
3366  3239  3239 ?        00:00:00         startplasma-x11
3373  3239  3239 ?        00:00:00           plasma_session
3423  3239  3239 ?        00:00:01             kded6
... many more lines omitted ...

As you can see, ps is an incredibly powerful tool for determining not only what processes are currently active on your system, but also for learning lots of important information about them.

As is the case with many of the applications, there is often several tools for the job. Similar to the ps -ejH output, but more terse, is pstree. It displays the process tree, a bit more visually.

darkstar:~$ pstree 
init─┬─ModemManager───3*[{ModemManager}]
     ├─NetworkManager─┬─dhcpcd─┬─dhcpcd───2*[dhcpcd]
     │                │        └─dhcpcd

     │                 ├─akonadi_indexin───2*[{akonadi_indexin}]
     │                 ├─akonadi_maildir───2*[{akonadi_maildir}]
     │                 ├─akonadi_maildis───3*[{akonadi_maildis}]
     │                 ├─akonadi_mailfil───4*[{akonadi_mailfil}]
     │                 ├─akonadi_mailmer───3*[{akonadi_mailmer}]
     │                 ├─akonadi_migrati───3*[{akonadi_migrati}]
     │                 ├─akonadi_newmail───3*[{akonadi_newmail}]
     │                 ├─akonadi_sendlat───3*[{akonadi_sendlat}]
     │                 ├─akonadi_unified───3*[{akonadi_unified}]
     │                 ├─akonadiserver─┬─mysqld───14*[{mysqld}]
     │                 │               └─26*[{akonadiserver}]
     │                 └─2*[{akonadi_control}]
... many more lines omitted ...

6.2 kill and killall

Managing processes isn't only about knowing which ones are running, but also about communicating with them to change their behavior. The most common way of managing a program is to terminate it. Thus, the tool for the job is named kill. Despite the name, kill doesn't actually terminate processes, but sends signals to them. The most common signal is SIGTERM, which tells the process to finish up what it is doing and terminate. There are a variety of other signals that can be sent, but the three most common are SIGTERM, SIGHUP, and SIGKILL.

What a process does when it receives a signal varies. Most programs will terminate (or attempt to terminate) whenever they receive any signal, but there are a few important differences. For starters, the SIGTERM signal informs the process that it should terminate itself at its earliest convenience. This gives the process time to finish up any important activities, such as writing information to disk, before it closes. In contrast, the SIGKILL signal tells the process to terminate itself immediately, no questions asked. This is most useful for killing processes that are not responding and is sometimes called the "silver bullet". Some processes (particularly daemons) capture the SIGHUP signal and reload their configuration files whenever they receive it.

In order to signal a process, we first need to know it's PID. You can get this easily with ps as we discused. In order to send different signals to a running process, you simply pass the signal name or number and -s as an argument. The -l argument lists all the signals you can choose and their number. You can also send signals by their name with -s .

darkstar:~$ kill -l 
 1) SIGHUP   2) SIGINT   3) SIGQUIT  4) SIGILL 
 5) SIGTRAP  6) SIGABRT  7) SIGBUS   8) SIGFPE 
 9) SIGKILL 10) SIGUSR1 11) SIGSEGV 12) SIGUSR2 
13) SIGPIPE 14) SIGALRM 15) SIGTERM 16) SIGSTKFLT 
... many more lines omitted ... 
darkstar:~$ kill 1234 # SIGTERM 
darkstar:~$ kill -s 9 1234 # SIGKILL 
darkstar:~$ kill -s 1 1234 # SIGHUP 
darkstar:~$ kill -s HUP 1234 # SIGHUP

Sometimes you may wish to terminate all running processes with a certain name. You can kill processes by name using killall. Just pass the same arguments to killall that you would pass to kill:

darkstar:~$ killall bash # SIGTERM 
darkstar:~$ killall -s 9 bash # SIGKILL 
darkstar:~$ killall -s 1 bash # SIGHUP 
darkstar:~$ killall -s HUP bash # SIGHUP

6.3 top

So far, we've learned how to look at active processes for a single moment in time, but what if we want to monitor them continuously over an extended period? top allows us to do just that. It displays an ordered, real-time list of active processes on your system along with vital resource consumption metrics, updating periodically (every three seconds by default).

The man page has helpful details on how to interact with top, such as changing its delay interval, altering the order in which processes are displayed, and even terminating processes right from within top itself.

6.4 htop

The htop like top allow you to look active processes in real-time and allows you to scroll vertically and horizontally, and interact using a pointing device (mouse). You can observe all processes running on the system, along with their command line arguments, as well as view them in a tree format, select multiple processes and act on them all at once.

At the top of the screen, htop features visual meters displaying real-time CPU, memory, and swap utilization. At the bottom, a footer bar displays available actions mapped to Function keys (F1 through F10). This allows you to search, filter, send signals (killing), or adjust process priorities (renicing) directly without needing to enter PIDs manually.

The man page has helpful details on how to interact with htop.

6.5 btop

Another popular resource monitor is btop, which displays real-time statistics and resource usage for the processor, memory, storage disks, network interfaces, and running processes. In other words, btop serves as a comprehensive, feature-rich TUI (Text User Interface) dashboard for system performance.

If you prefer an intuitive, visually responsive interface to monitor your system, btop is an excellent choice. It features full mouse support, allowing you to click on elements, scroll through lists, and select individual PIDs to view detailed process trees or filter resource usage effortlessly.

The man page has helpful details on how to interact with btop.

6.6 cron

So far, we've learned many different ways of viewing active processes on our system and methods for signaling them, but what if we want to run a process periodically on an automated schedule? Fortunately, Slackware includes just the tool for the job: crond. cron runs processes for every user on a schedule specified by that user. This makes it extremely useful for tasks that need to run periodically without requiring a dedicated daemon, such as automated backup scripts. Every user gets their own entry in the cron database, allowing non-root users to schedule their own background tasks as well.

In order to manage scheduled tasks, you will need to use the crontab utility. The man page lists a variety of options, but the most common method is using the -e argument. This locks the user's entry in the cron database (preventing concurrent modifications) and opens it in the text editor specified by the VISUAL or EDITOR environment variable. On Slackware systems, this defaults to the vi editor. You may wish to review the chapter on vi before proceeding.

The cron database entries may seem a bit archaic at first glance, but they are remarkably flexible. Each uncommented line is processed by crond, and the specified command is executed whenever all time conditions match.

darkstar:~$ crontab -e 
#Keep current with slackware 
30 02 * * * /usr/local/bin/rsync-slackware64.sh 1>/dev/null 2>&1

As mentioned before, the syntax for cron entries can be a little difficult to understand at first, so let's break down each part individually. From left to right, the five time fields are: Minute, Hour, Day of Month, Month, and Day of Week, followed by the Command to execute.

An asterisk (*) acts as a wildcard, matching every possible value for that field (e.g., every minute, every hour, or every day).

In the previous example (30 02 * * * /usr/local/bin/rsync-slackware64.sh 1>/dev/null 2>&1): * Minute (30): At minute 30 * Hour (02): At 2:00 AM * Day of Month (*): Every day of the month * Month (*): Every month * Day of Week (*): Every day of the week

Therefore, the command /usr/local/bin/rsync-slackware64.sh 1>/dev/null 2>&1 runs every day at 2:30 a.m.

crond will also email the local user with any standard output or error generated by a executed command. For this reason, many tasks have their output redirected to /dev/null, a special character device file that immediately discards any data written to it.

To make it easier to remember the syntax rules and output redirection, you can paste the following commented template at the top of your own crontab file:

# MIN HOUR DAY MONTH WEEKDAY COMMAND
# Redirect output to /dev/null: 1>/dev/null 2>&1

By default, Slackware includes a number of pre-configured entries and comments in root's crontab. These entries make it easier to set up periodic system maintenance by utilizing dedicated directories in /etc corresponding to execution frequencies. Any executable script placed within these directories will automatically run on that schedule.

The directory names are self-explanatory: * /etc/cron.hourly — Executed once every hour * /etc/cron.daily — Executed once every day * /etc/cron.weekly — Executed once every week * /etc/cron.monthly — Executed once every month

6.7 at

Similar to cron, the at utility allows you to schedule tasks for automatic execution, but with a key difference: while cron is designed for recurring, periodic jobs, at executes a scheduled task only once at a specified future time.

To schedule a task, you specify the time as an argument. You can either enter commands interactively (pressing Ctrl+d when finished) or specify a script file directly using the -f option.

Following the previous update script example, to schedule a single execution at 10:35 AM:

darkstar:~$ at 10:35 -f /usr/local/bin/rsync-slackware64.sh
warning: commands will be executed using /bin/sh
job 1 at Wed Oct 29 10:35:00 2026

To schedule a task for 10:35 PM, you can specify:

darkstar:~$ at 10:35PM -f /usr/local/bin/rsync-slackware64.sh
warning: commands will be executed using /bin/sh
job 2 at Wed Oct 29 22:35:00 2026

To view currently scheduled jobs, use the -l option:

darkstar:~$ at -l
2       Tue Oct 29 22:45:00 2026 a brusso
1       Wed Oct 28 10:45:00 2026 a brusso

Note: An alternative to at -l is the standalone atq command.

To remove a scheduled job, pass its job number using the -r option (or use the equivalent atrm command):

darkstar:~$ at -r 1
2       Tue Oct 29 22:45:00 2026 a brusso
1       Wed Oct 28 10:45:00 2026 a brusso


darkstar:~$ at -l
2       Tue Oct 29 22:45:00 2026 a brusso

Another useful feature of at is its flexible time specification syntax—it supports natural expressions such as now + 3 hours, noon, or tomorrow. Consult the man page for at for full details on supported time formats and interaction options.

7. The X Window System

7.1 What Is (And Isn't) X

Eons ago computer terminals came with a screen and a keyboard and not much else. Mice hadn't come into common use and everything was menu driven. Then came the Graphical User Interface (GUI) and the world was changed. Today users are accustomed to moving a mouse around a screen, clicking on icons and running tasks with fancy images and animation, but UNIX systems predated this and so GUIs were added almost as an afterthought. For many years, Linux and its UNIX brethren were primarily used without graphics of any sort, but today it is perhaps more common than not for users to prefer their Linux computers come with shiney, flashy, clickable GUIs, and all these GUIs run on X (7).

So what is X? Is it the desktop with the icons? Is it the menus? Is it the window manager? Does it mark the spot? The answer to all these is a resounding "no". There are many parts to a GUI, but X is the most fundamental. X is that application that receives input from the mouse, keyboard, and possibly other devices. X is that application that tells the graphics card what to do. In short, X is the application that talks to your computer's hardware for graphical purposes; all other graphical applications simply talk to X.

Let's stop for a moment and talk about nomenclature. X is just one of a dozen names that you may encounter. It is also called X11, xorg, the X Window System, X Window, X11R6, X Version 11, and several others. Whatever you hear it called, simply understand that the speakers are referring to X.

7.2 Configuring the X Server

Once upon a time, configuring X was a difficult and painful process that caused the magic smoke to come gushing out of hundreds of monitors. Today X is a lot more user friendly. In fact, most users will not need to configure X at all, Slackware will simply figure out all the proper settings on its own. There are, however, still some computers that X can't properly auto-configure and will need a little bit of work on your part.

Once upon a time, the X configuration file was located at /etc/X11/xorg.conf , and if you create a file there, X will honor whatever settings you place within it. Fortunately, with X.Org 1.6.3 an /etc/X11/xorg.conf does not even need to be present for X to generate a working display. If for whatever reason, you need to make configuration changes to X, try to avoid using this file; it's antiquated and inflexible. Rather, the /etc/X11/xorg.conf.d/ directory is where you should put such tweaks. Any file you place within that directory will be read when X starts up. This allows you to split-up your configuration into more easily manageable parts. For example, here's my /etc/X11/xorg.conf.d/synaptics.conf file for my laptop.


darkstar:~$ cat /etc/X11/xorg.conf.d/synaptics.conf 
Section "InputDevice"
    Identifier      "Synaptics Touchpad"
    Driver          "synaptics"
    Option          "SendCoreEvents"        "true"
    Option          "Device"                "/dev/psaux"
    Option          "Protocol"              "auto-dev"
    Option          "SHMConfig"             "on"
    Option          "LeftEdge"              "100"
    Option          "RightEdge"             "1120"
    Option          "TopEdge"               "50"
    Option          "BottomEdge"            "310"
    Option          "FingerLow"             "25"
    Option          "FingerHigh"            "30"
    Option          "VertScrollDelta"       "20"
    Option          "HorizScrollDelta"      "50"
    Option          "MinSpeed"              "0.79"
    Option          "MaxSpeed"              "0.88"
    Option          "AccelFactor"           "0.0015"
    Option          "TapButton1"            "1"
    Option          "TapButton2"            "2"
    Option          "TapButton3"            "3"
    Option          "MaxTapMove"            "100"
    Option          "HorizScrollDelta"      "0"
    Option          "HorizEdgeScroll"       "0"
    Option          "VertEdgeScroll"        "1"
    Option          "VertTwoFingerScroll"   "0"
EndSection

By placing such options in individual files, you can easily manage your X configuration by sections.

7.3 Choosing a Window Manager

Slackware Linux includes many different window managers and desktop environments. Window managers are the applications responsible for painting application windows on the screen, resizing these windows, and similar tasks. Desktop environments include a window manager, but also add task bars, menus, icons, and more. Slackware includes both the KDE and XFCE desktop environments and several additional window managers. Which you use is entirely your own decision, but in general, window managers tend to be faster than desktop environments and more suitable to older systems with less memory and slower processors. Desktop environments will be more comfortable for users accustomed to Microsoft Windows.

The easiest way to choose a window manager is xwmconfig (1), included with Slackware Linux. This application allows a user to choose what window manager to run with startx .

7.4 Setting Up A Graphical Login

By default, when you boot your Slackware Linux system you are presented with a login prompt on a virtual terminal. This is more than adequate for most people's needs. If you need to run commandline applications, you may login and do so right away. If you want to run X, simply executing startx will do that for you nicely. But suppose you almost exclusively use your system for graphical duties like many laptop owners? Wouldn't it be nice for Slackware to take you straight into a GUI? Fortunately, there's an easy way to do just that.

Slackware uses the System V init system which allows the administrator to boot into or change to different runlevels, which are really just different "states" the computer can be in. In fact, shutting down the computer is really only a case of changing to a runlevel which accomplishes just that. Runlevels can be rather complicated, so we won't delve into them any further than necessary.

Runlevels are configured in inittab (5). The most common ones are runlevel 3 (Slackware's default) and runlevel 4 (GUI). In order to tell Slackware to boot to a GUI screen, simply open /etc/inittab with your favorite editor of choice. (You may wish to refer to one of the chapters on vi or emacs at this point.) Near the top, you'll see the relevant entries.


#These are the default runlevels in Slackware: 
#0 = halt 
#1 = single user mode 
#2 = unused (but configured the same as runlevel 3) 
#3 = multiuser mode (default Slackware runlevel) 
#4 = X11 with KDM/GDM/XDM (session managers) 
#5 = unused (but configured the same as runlevel 3) 
#6 = reboot 

#Default runlevel. (Do not set to 0 or 6) 
id:3:initdefault:

In this file (along with most configuration files) anything following a hash symbol # is a comment and not interpreted by init(8). Don't worry if you don't understand everything about inittab, as many veteran users don't either. The only line we are interested in is the last on above. Simply change the 3 to a 4 and reboot.


#These are the default runlevels in Slackware: 
#0 = halt 
#1 = single user mode 
#2 = unused (but configured the same as runlevel 3) 
#3 = multiuser mode (default Slackware runlevel) 
#4 = X11 with KDM/GDM/XDM (session managers) 
#5 = unused (but configured the same as runlevel 3) 
#6 = reboot 

#Default runlevel. (Do not set to 0 or 6) 
id:4:initdefault:

8. Printing

8.1 Choosing A Printer

Historically, printer configuration on Linux required navigating complex spoolers and custom filter scripts, making hardware support hit-or-miss. Today, the vast majority of modern printers work seamlessly with Linux out of the box, although hardware compatibility can still vary depending on the manufacturer and model.

When selecting a new printer for a Slackware environment:

  • Laser Printers: Generally offer the best out-of-the-box compatibility, particularly models supporting standard printer command languages like PostScript or PCL.
  • Inkjet Printers: Entry-level or budget inkjet models often rely on proprietary host-based rasterization protocols ("Winprinters") that may lack native Linux driver support.

If you are uncertain about hardware compatibility prior to purchase, consult community resources such as OpenPrinting.org to verify driver availability and user reports.

The Common UNIX Printing System (CUPS)

Slackware manages printing services using CUPS (Common UNIX Printing System), the industry-standard printing environment across UNIX-like operating systems. CUPS manages print queues, rasterizes documents, converts job formats (such as PDF or PostScript), and communicates with physical local and network devices.

CUPS features a built-in web-based administration interface that allows users and administrators to add printers, configure options, and monitor active print jobs.

Accessing the CUPS Web Interface

To configure a printer using CUPS, launch any web browser (such as firefox or console browsers like links) and navigate to:

http://localhost:631

8.2 Getting the Driver

Before configuring a printer in CUPS, you must ensure the appropriate driver or description file is available on your system. Printer drivers generally fall into three main categories:

  1. PostScript Printers: PostScript printers use the universal PostScript page description language to communicate with computers. A dedicated driver is usually unnecessary because Slackware includes Ghostscript, a PostScript-compatible subsystem, out of the box.
  2. Gutenprint Drivers: Gutenprint is an open-source driver suite developed for Linux that provides support for hundreds of printer models.
  3. Manufacturer-Provided Drivers: Printer manufacturers often provide Linux-specific drivers on their official support websites for models that require proprietary rasterizers.

Installing Drivers in Slackware

Because Gutenprint is pre-installed in Slackware, driver setup generally follows one of two methods:

  • Manufacturer Drivers: Installation is handled like any standard software package in Slackware. Download the package from the manufacturer and install it using installpkg (it may be necessary to convert RPM packages using rpm2tgz first). Always consult the documentation supplied with the driver package for special setup requirements.
  • PostScript PPD Files: For PostScript printers, no formal driver installation is required. Simply download the appropriate PPD (PostScript Printer Description) file for your model and save it in a sensible directory on your filesystem.

Note: In my environment, I use an HP Photosmart printer, whose drivers are installed by default on Slackware.

Once you have identified and installed or downloaded the necessary driver components, you are ready to configure the printer in CUPS.

8.3 Setting Up a Printer in CUPS

While CUPS manages its printer definitions behind the scenes in /etc/cups/printers.conf, the easiest and most straightforward way to configure a printer is through the built-in CUPS web administration interface.

Step-by-Step Web Interface Setup

  1. Access the Administration Page: Open any web browser (such as Firefox or links) and navigate to: http://localhost:631. Click on the Administration tab at the top of the page, then select Add Printer.

  2. Authenticate as Root: When prompted for administrative credentials, enter root as the username along with your system's root password.

  3. Select the Printer Connection / Protocol: CUPS will display a list of available local interfaces and network protocols: * Local Printer: If your printer is connected directly via USB and powered on, it will appear under Local Printers. * Network Printer: For networked printers, select the appropriate connection protocol (most commonly LPD/LPR Host or Printer, AppSocket/HP JetDirect, or IPP).

  4. Specify the Device Location / URI (Network Printers Only): If configuring a network printer, enter its IP address or network URI. For example, using the LPD/LPR protocol:
    lpd://192.168.4.8
    (You can locate your printer's IP address on its status screen or from your router's device list).

  5. Enter Printer Details: Provide human-readable information to identify the device: * Name: A short, single-word identifier without spaces (e.g., hp or office_hp). * Description: A distinctive description (e.g., HP Photosmart). * Location: The physical placement of the machine (e.g., Downstairs Office).

  6. Select the Printer Driver or PPD File:
    Point CUPS to the appropriate printer model definition: * Manufacturer List: Select the manufacturer (e.g., Epson or HP) and choose your model driver from the list (such as HP Photosmart c4440 Series, hpcups 3.26.6 (en)). * PPD File: If you downloaded a custom .ppd file, click Browse... to upload the PPD directly.

  7. Save and Set Default Options:
    Click Add Printer, set default options (paper size, print quality, duplexing), and confirm. Your printer is now ready to receive print jobs.

Under the Hood: How CUPS Stores Configuration When you complete the web setup, CUPS automatically generates an entry in /etc/cups/printers.conf defining attributes like MakeModel and DeviceURI (e.g., lpd://192.168.4.8). While manual editing of /etc/cups/printers.conf is possible, it requires stopping the cupsd daemon first (/etc/rc.d/rc.cups stop) to prevent configuration overwrites. Using the web interface handles daemon updates safely and automatically.

darkstar:~# cat /etc/cups/printers.conf
# Printer configuration file for CUPS v2.4.20
# Written by cupsd
# DO NOT EDIT THIS FILE WHEN CUPSD IS RUNNING
NextPrinterId 2
<DefaultPrinter Photosmart_C4400>
PrinterId 1
UUID urn:uuid:6c68e0ee-e32c-3a28-48a1-92bc88b33070
Info
Location
MakeModel HP Photosmart c4400 Series, hpcups 3.26.6
DeviceURI hp:/usb/Photosmart_C4400_series?serial=BR8BTFD0NW05BW
State Idle
StateTime 1795197275
ConfigTime 1785366850
Type 36892
Accepting Yes
Shared Yes
JobSheets none none
QuotaPeriod 0
PageLimit 0
KLimit 0
OpPolicy default
ErrorPolicy stop-printer
</DefaultPrinter>

As shown in the screenshot below, the newly configured printer is now visible and active within the CUPS web administration interface, displaying its status as Idle and ready to accept print jobs:

8.4 Printing from the Command Line

Now that you have successfully installed and configured your printer, you can print documents directly from the terminal using lpr.

The lpr utility sends files to the print queue. Before printing, it is helpful to set a default printer on your system so you do not have to specify the target printer name with every command.

Setting the Default Printer

Method 1: System-Wide (as Root) To set a system-wide default printer for all users on the machine, run lpadmin with the -d flag as root:

darkstar:~# lpadmin -d Photosmart_C4400

Note: In this example, Photosmart_C4400 is the queue name defined during the CUPS setup (either in the web interface or /etc/cups/printers.conf).

Method 2: Per-User (Environment Variable) If you do not have root privileges on the workstation, you can set the default printer for your user account by exporting the PRINTER environment variable in your shell:

darkstar:~\$ export PRINTER=Photosmart_C4400

To make this setting permanent for your user account, add export PRINTER=Photosmart_C4400 to your ~/.bashrc configuration file.

Sending Jobs to the Printer

Once a default printer is configured, send any document to the print queue simply by passing the filename to lpr:

darkstar:~\$ lpr document.txt

If you have multiple printers configured and wish to send a document to a specific printer other than your default, use the -P flag:

darkstar:~\$ lpr -P Photosmart_C4400 document.txt

8.4.1 Formatting for Print with pr

Like many traditional UNIX utilities, lpr adheres strictly to the UNIX philosophy: it does one thing—sending raw data to a print queue—without altering document layout, margins, or pagination. When printing unformatted plain text files, sending raw text directly to a printer can result in awkward page breaks, missing margins, or text running off the physical page boundaries.

To format plain text documents before printing, use pr.

pr is a standard UNIX text formatting utility that prepares plain text files for printing by inserting proper page breaks, headers (including the timestamp, file name, and page number), footers, and margins. Because pr outputs its formatted text directly to standard output (stdout), it seamlessly integrates with lpr via standard shell pipelines.

Piping Formatted Text to lpr

To format a text file and send the output directly to your default print queue in a single command, pipe the output of pr into lpr:

darkstar:~\$ pr document.txt | lpr

You can also customize the header title and layout options before sending the job to the printer:

darkstar:~\$ pr -h "Quarterly Report" -l 60 document.txt | lpr

Common pr Command Flags

Flag Description Example
-h
Replaces the default header filename with a custom title string. pr -h "System Log" log.txt
-l Sets the page length in lines (default is 66 lines per page). pr -l 60 document.txt
-w Sets the page width in columns (default is 72 columns). pr -w 80 document.txt
-d Double-spaces the text output. pr -d document.txt
-n Prepends line numbers to each line of formatted text. pr -n document.txt
-m Formats and prints multiple files side-by-side in parallel columns. pr -m file1.txt file2.txt

8.5 Printing from Graphical Applications

Once a printer is configured in CUPS, it is instantly available across all Desktop Environments (such as KDE Plasma or XFCE) and graphical applications running on Slackware.

Modern Linux applications rely on standardized GTK and Qt print dialog backends that communicate directly with the local CUPS daemon (cupsd).

Using standard Print Dialogs (Ctrl+P)

Whether you are editing a document in LibreOffice, viewing a PDF in Okular, or browsing the web in Firefox, sending a document to the printer follows a unified workflow:

  1. Open the Print Dialog: Press Ctrl + P (or navigate to File → Print in the application menu).
  2. Select the Printer: Choose your configured printer queue (e.g., Photosmart_C4400) from the destination list.
  3. Adjust Print Options: Graphical dialogs automatically query CUPS for the printer's PPD capabilities, allowing you to configure: * Page Range & Copies: Print specific pages or collate multiple copies. * Color & Quality: Toggle between monochrome (grayscale) and full color, or adjust print resolution. * Layout & Duplex: Select paper orientation (Portrait/Landscape) and enable double-sided (duplex) printing if supported by the hardware.
  4. Print: Click Print to send the processed document to the CUPS queue.

Because CUPS handles queue management globally, print jobs submitted from graphical applications appear alongside CLI jobs in the CUPS web interface status page (http://localhost:631/jobs).

9. Users and Groups

9.1 What Are Users and Groups?

Slackware Linux inherits a robust multi-user architecture from its UNIX design principles. In a multi-user environment, multiple users can operate the system simultaneously, each isolated by distinct security permissions. This boundary prevents unauthorized access to or modification of other users' files and enables system administrators to define precise access controls across the entire system.

Furthermore, user accounts in Slackware are not limited to human operators. The operating system includes numerous pre-defined system users and groups (such as daemon, nobody, bin, and ssdm). Rather than facilitating interactive logins, these specialized accounts serve to segment system daemons, restrict service privileges, and enforce the principle of least privilege—a topic explored in detail in the next chapter on filesystem permissions.

Note on Local vs. Centralized Authentication: While Slackware Linux can be configured to integrate with centralized authentication directories (such as LDAP, NIS, or Kerberos), the commands and examples in this chapter focus exclusively on local user administration, where account information is stored directly in /etc/passwd, /etc/shadow, and /etc/group.

9.2 Managing Users and Groups

Slackware provides both interactive helper scripts for quick administration and powerful low-level command-line utilities for automation and batch operations.

9.2.1 Interactive Account Creation (adduser)

The simplest and most user-friendly way to create a new user account in Slackware is using the interactive adduser shell script. adduser guides you through each configuration step—setting up the username, UID, initial group, supplementary groups, home directory, login shell, and initial password.

darkstar:~# adduser 

Login name for new user []: alan

User ID ('UID') [ defaults to next available ]: 

Initial group [ users ]: 
Additional UNIX groups:

Users can belong to additional UNIX groups on the system.
root@maria:/home/brusso# adduser 

Login name for new user []: alan

User ID ('UID') [ defaults to next available ]: 

Initial group [ users ]: users
Additional UNIX groups:

Users can belong to additional UNIX groups on the system.
root@maria:/home/brusso# adduser 

Login name for new user []: alan

User ID ('UID') [ defaults to next available ]: 

Initial group [ users ]: users
Additional UNIX groups:

Users can belong to additional UNIX groups on the system.
For local users using graphical desktop login managers such
as XDM/KDM, users may need to be members of additional groups
to access the full functionality of removable media devices.

* Security implications *
Please be aware that by adding users to additional groups may
potentially give access to the removable media of other users.

If you are creating a new user for remote shell access only,
users do not need to belong to any additional groups as standard,
so you may press ENTER at the next prompt.

Press ENTER to continue without adding any additional groups
Or press the UP arrow key to add/select/edit additional groups
:  cdrom floppy plugdev

Home directory [ /home/alan ] 

Press the UP and DOWN arrow keys to cycle through the available shells
Shell [ /bin/bash ] 

Expiry date (YYYY-MM-DD) []: 

New account will be created as follows:

---------------------------------------
Login name.......:  alan
UID..............:  [ Next available ]
Initial group....:  users
Additional groups:  cdrom,floppy,plugdev
Home directory...:  /home/alan
Shell............:  /bin/bash
Expiry date......:  [ Never ]

This is it... if you want to bail out, hit Control-C.  Otherwise, press
ENTER to go ahead and make the account.


Creating new account...


Changing finger information for alan.
Name []: Alan Hicks
Office []: SlackwareBook
Work phone []:
Home phone []:


Finger information has been changed.
New password:
Retype new password:
passwd: password updated successfully

Account setup complete.

9.2.2 Supplementary Groups for Desktop Functionality

Group membership in Slackware controls access to hardware devices and system services. Every user has a primary group (by default, users), but accounts can belong to multiple secondary groups to inherit additional system permissions:

  • audio: Grants access to sound cards and audio hardware.
  • video: Grants access to GPU acceleration and display devices.
  • cdrom / floppy / plugdev: Enables mounting and managing removable storage devices (USB drives, optical discs) without requiring root privileges.

During the adduser prompt, pressing the Up Arrow key automatically inserts Slackware's recommended default groups for interactive desktop users.

9.2.3 Non-Interactive User Creation (useradd)

While adduser is ideal for interactive use, useradd is the low-level utility used for non-interactive account creation, shell scripting, and batch operations. In fact, adduser itself is a wrapper script built on top of useradd.

Unlike adduser, useradd requires explicit command-line flags to set non-default attributes:

darkstar:~# useradd -d /home/alan -s /bin/bash -g users -G cdrom,floppy,plugdev alan

In this example: * -d /home/alan: Sets a custom path for the home directory. * -s /bin/bash: Assigns /bin/bash as the default login shell. * -g users: Sets the primary group to users. * -G audio,cdrom,...: Specifies a comma-separated list of secondary groups.

Note: useradd creates the user account but does not set a password. You must run passwd username immediately afterward to enable password authentication for the new account.

9.2.4 Managing Groups (groupadd)

Creating new security groups is handled by groupadd. Like useradd, it operates non-interactively:

darkstar:~# groupadd slackers

This creates a new system group named slackers and automatically assigns it the next available GID (Group ID) in /etc/group.

9.2.5 Deleting Accounts and Groups (userdel and groupdel)

Removing accounts and groups is straightforward using userdel and groupdel:

darkstar:~# userdel -r alan
darkstar:~# groupdel slackers
  • userdel alan: Removes the user account entry from /etc/passwd and /etc/shadow, but leaves the user's home directory (/home/alan) intact on disk.
  • userdel -r alan: Removes both the user account and recursively deletes their home directory and mail spool.
  • groupdel slackers: Removes the specified group entry from /etc/group.

9.3 Other User and Group Tools

In addition to account creation and deletion tools, Slackware includes several command-line utilities for modifying existing user attributes, managing passwords, and updating user environment settings.

9.3.1 Managing Passwords and Account Locks (passwd)

The most fundamental credential utility is passwd. Regular users can execute passwd without arguments to update their own login password. The system administrator (root) can change the password for any user by specifying the target username:

darkstar:~# passwd alan

Locking and Unlocking Accounts

The root user can temporarily disable logins for a specific account using the -l (lock) flag:

darkstar:~# passwd -l alan

This operation locks the account by prepending an exclamation mark (!) to the encrypted password string in /etc/shadow, preventing password-based authentication without deleting user files or home directories. To restore access, execute passwd with the -u (unlock) flag:

darkstar:~# passwd -u alan

9.3.2 Modifying Existing User Accounts (usermod)

The usermod utility modifies existing user account settings in /etc/passwd and /etc/shadow. It is the primary command for altering group memberships, home directory locations, and default login shells.

Appending vs. Overwriting Secondary Groups (-a -G)

When managing secondary group memberships with usermod, understanding the distinction between appending and overwriting groups is critical:

Correct: Appends "lp" to existing secondary groups

darkstar:~# usermod -a -G lp alan

CAUTION: Overwrites ALL secondary groups with ONLY "lp"

darkstar:~# usermod -G lp alan
  • usermod -a -G lp alan: The -a (append) option ensures that alan is added to the lp group while preserving all current secondary group memberships (such as audio, video, plugdev).
  • usermod -G lp alan: Omitting the -a flag replaces all of alan's secondary group memberships with only lp, revoking access to all previously assigned groups.

Common usermod Operations: * -d /new/home -m: Updates the home directory path and moves existing files to the new location. * -s /bin/zsh: Changes the user's default login shell. * -l newname: Renames the user account login name. * -e YYYY-MM-DD: Sets an explicit expiration date for the account.

9.3.3 Changing Default Shells (chsh)

The chsh (change shell) utility allows users to modify their default login shell without needing administrative intervention:

darkstar:~\$ chsh -s /bin/zsh

Regular users can only select valid system shells listed in /etc/shells. The root user can change any user's shell by passing the target username to chsh.

9.3.4 Updating User Information (chfn)

The chfn (change finger) utility updates user identity information stored in the GECOS field of /etc/passwd (such as full name, office room, and phone numbers):

darkstar:~\$ chfn

This metadata can be queried locally or retrieved over networks using utilities like finger.

9.4 Managing Users and Groups Manually

Like most configuration aspects in Slackware Linux, user and group definitions are stored as plain-text files on the local filesystem. While higher-level tools like adduser and usermod automate account management, you can create, modify, or inspect user accounts directly by editing these configuration files and manually setting up home directories.

9.4.1 Structure of /etc/passwd

The primary account registry is /etc/passwd. This file contains one line per user account, with seven distinct fields separated by colons (:):

darkstar:~\$ cat /etc/passwd | grep alan
alan:x:1002:100:Alan Hicks,Slackware Book:/home/alan:/bin/zsh

From left to right, the fields represent: 1. Username (alan): The unique login name for the account. 2. Password Placeholder (x): Historical password field. The literal x indicates that the encrypted password string is safely stored in /etc/shadow. 3. User ID / UID (1000): The numerical identifier assigned to the user by the kernel. 4. Group ID / GID (100): The numerical identifier of the user's primary group (by default, GID 100 corresponds to users). 5. GECOS / Comment Field (Alan Hicks,,,): Optional metadata, such as full name, office location, and contact numbers (managed via chfn). 6. Home Directory (/home/alan): The absolute path to the user's initial working directory upon login. 7. Default Shell (/bin/bash): The path to the command interpreter executed when the user logs in.

9.4.2 Shadow Password Security (/etc/shadow)

Because /etc/passwd must remain world-readable (so system programs can map UIDs to usernames), storing password hashes inside it presents a severe security risk. Slackware uses shadow password protection, moving encrypted hashes to /etc/shadow, which is readable only by root (permissions 0600).

A typical entry in /etc/shadow looks like this:

darkstar:~\# cat /etc/shadow | grep alan
alan:$6$h2sjEdiuwzO.R13H$cZfZjZO9imd4uqfJKal/2cdbLZu9e0EgDWoR96QuKdUTQ.dY7FJhy0:20775:0:99999:7:::

The fields in /etc/shadow specify:

  1. Username: Matches the corresponding account in /etc/passwd.
  2. Encrypted Password: The cryptographic password hash (typically SHA-512 in modern Slackware). An ! or * in this field locks the account.
  3. Last Change Date: The date the password was last modified, expressed as the number of days elapsed since the Unix Epoch (January 1, 1970).
  4. Minimum Password Age: Minimum number of days required before the password can be changed again.
  5. Maximum Password Age: Maximum number of days the password remains valid before forcing a password change.
  6. Warning Period: Number of days prior to password expiration that the user is warned to change credentials.
  7. Inactivity Period: Days after password expiration during which the account remains accessible before being automatically disabled.
  8. Account Expiration Date: Absolute date (in days since Epoch) when the account expires.
  9. Reserved Field: Reserved for future use.

9.4.3 Group Definitions (/etc/group)

Group relationships are maintained in /etc/group. Each line defines a system group using four colon-separated fields:

darkstar:~\# cat /etc/group | grep alan
floppy:x:11:alan
cdrom:x:19:alan
plugdev:x:83:alan
  • Group Name (wheel): The unique string identifier for the group.
  • Group Password Placeholder (x): Placeholder for optional group passwords (stored in /etc/gshadow).
  • Group ID / GID (10): The unique numerical GID.
  • Member List (root,alan): A comma-separated list of secondary users assigned to this group.

9.4.4 Safe Manual Editing with vipw and vigr

Directly opening /etc/passwd or /etc/shadow with a standard text editor like vi or nano is dangerous, as concurrent writes can corrupt the system database.

Slackware provides dedicated locking utilities for manual editing:

  • vipw: Safely opens /etc/passwd with mandatory file locking.
  • vipw -s: Safely opens /etc/shadow with file locking.
  • vigr: Safely opens /etc/group with file locking.
  • vigr -s: Safely opens /etc/gshadow with file locking.

These tools respect your EDITOR or VISUAL environment variables and prevent race conditions by locking the files during editing.

9.4.5 Complete Manual Account Setup Workflow

Creating a user manually requires more than adding text entries to configuration files. To fully set up a functional user account manually, follow these steps:

  1. Lock and Edit Account Files: Run vipw to add the user entry to /etc/passwd, then run vipw -s to initialize the shadow entry.
  2. Assign Group Memberships: Run vigr to add the username to appropriate secondary groups in /etc/group.
  3. Create the Home Directory: Create the user's home directory and populate it with default profile templates from /etc/skel/:
darkstar:~# mkdir -p /home/alan
darkstar:~# cp -r /etc/skel/.[a-z]* /home/alan/
  • Set Correct Ownership and Permissions: Assign ownership of the home directory and its contents to the new user and primary group:
darkstar:~# chown -R alan:users /home/alan
darkstar:~# chmod 701 /home/alan
  • Set Initial Password: Initialize the account password using the passwd utility:
darkstar:~# passwd alan

10. Filesystem Permissions

10.1 Permissions Overview

As we have discussed, Slackware Linux is a multi-user operating system. Because of this, its filesystems are multi-user as well. This means that every file or directory has a set of permissions that grant or deny privileges to different users. There are three basic permissions (read, write, execute) and three permission sets for every file or directory.

Let's examine an example file using ls -l:

darkstar:~$ ls -l /bin/ls 
-rwxr-xr-x 1 root root 163384 Oct 24 20:46 /bin/ls

Recall from Chapter 4 that ls -l displays permissions along with the user and group that own the file. In this example:

  • The leading - indicates a regular file.
  • The permission string is rwxr-xr-x.
  • The user owner is root and the group owner is also root.

While displayed together, the 9-character permission string is divided into three separate triplets:

Set Listing Meaning
Owner rwx The owner "root" may read, write, and execute
Group r-x The group "root" may read and execute
Others r-x Everyone else may read and execute

Permissions behave slightly differently depending on whether they apply to a file or a directory:

  • Read (r): On a file, allows reading its contents. On a directory, allows listing its contained files (e.g., using ls).
  • Write (w): On a file, allows modifying or truncating its contents. On a directory, allows creating, deleting, or renaming files within it.
  • Execute (x): On a file, allows running it as a program or script. On a directory, allows entering the directory (e.g., using cd) and accessing its subdirectories or file metadata.

Let's inspect the permissions on a user's home directory using ls -ld:

darkstar:~$ ls -ld /home/brusso
drwx--x--- 114 brusso users 4096 Oct 28 11:57 /home/brusso/

Here, the leading d indicates a directory. The directory is owned by the user brusso and the group users:

  • Owner (brusso): Granted full access (rwx).
  • Group (users): Granted read and execute permissions (r-x).
  • Others: Prohibited from accessing, listing, or entering the directory (---).

10.2 chmod, chown, and chgrp

Now that we understand what permissions are and how they are structured, how do we change them? And how do we assign user and group ownership? This section covers the core command-line tools used to manage filesystem security and access rights.

The primary tool for modifying file ownership is chown (change owner). Traditionally used to change user ownership, chown can also modify group ownership simultaneously.

Let's look at an example:

darkstar:~# ls -l /tmp/foo
total 0
-rw-r--r-- 1 brusso users 0 nov  2 18:51 a
-rw-r--r-- 1 brusso users 0 nov  2 18:51 b
darkstar:~# chown root /tmp/foo/a
darkstar:~# ls -l /tmp/foo
total 0
-rw-r--r-- 1 root   users 0 nov  2 18:51 a
-rw-r--r-- 1 brusso users 0 nov  2 18:51 b

By separating a username and group name with a colon (:), chown can change both user and group ownership in a single step:

darkstar:~# chown root:root /tmp/foo/b
darkstar:~# ls -l /tmp/foo
total 0
-rw-r--r-- 1 root users 0 Nov  2 18:51 a
-rw-r--r-- 1 root root  0 Nov  2 18:51 b

If you specify a colon followed by a group name without a username, chown changes only the group ownership while leaving the user owner unchanged:

darkstar:~# chown :wheel /tmp/foo/a
darkstar:~# ls -l /tmp/foo
total 0
-rw-r--r-- 1 root wheel 0 Nov  2 18:51 a
-rw-r--r-- 1 root root  0 Nov  2 18:51 b

To change the ownership of a directory and all of its contents (files and subdirectories) recursively, use the -R flag:

darkstar:~# chown -R root:root /tmp/foo
darkstar:~# ls -l /tmp/foo
total 0
-rw-r--r-- 1 root root 0 nov  2 18:51 a
-rw-r--r-- 1 root root 0 nov  2 18:51 b

The chgrp (change group) utility is a dedicated tool used exclusively to change group ownership:

darkstar:~# chgrp wheel /tmp/foo/a
darkstar:~# ls -l /tmp/foo
total 0
-rw-r--r-- 1 root wheel 0 nov  2 18:51 a
-rw-r--r-- 1 root root  0 nov  2 18:51 b

While chown can already change group ownership, chgrp exists for POSIX compliance and compatibility with other UNIX variants whose legacy chown tools did not support group modifications.

Changing Permissions (chmod)

The command used to modify file and directory access permissions is chmod (change mode). Only the file's owner or the root superuser can change its permissions.

chmod supports two methods for defining permissions: Octal (Numeric) Mode and Symbolic (Alphabetic) Mode.

1. Octal (Numeric) Mode

In octal mode, permissions are represented by a 3-digit number. Each digit corresponds to one of the permission sets in order: Owner, Group, and Others.

Each basic permission has a specific numeric value based on powers of 2:

Permission Octal Value Bit Position
Read (r) 4 2^2
Write (w) 2 2^1
Execute (x) 1 2^0

By adding these values together, you can specify any permission combination from 0 (no permissions) to 7 (full read, write, and execute permissions):

Value Permissions String Meaning
0 None --- No access
1 Execute only --x Execute
2 Write only -w- Write
3 Write + Execute -wx Write & Execute (2 + 1)
4 Read only r-- Read
5 Read + Execute r-x Read & Execute (4 + 1)
6 Read + Write rw- Read & Write (4 + 2)
7 Read + Write + Execute rwx Read, Write & Execute (4 + 2 + 1)

For example, to set permissions to rwxr-x--- (User: 7, Group: 5, Others: 0):

darkstar:~# ls -l /tmp/foo/a
-rw-r--r-- 1 root root 0 Nov  2 18:51 a
darkstar:~# chmod 750 /tmp/foo/a
darkstar:~# ls -l /tmp/foo/a
-rwxr-x--- 1 root root 0 Nov  2 18:51 a

2. Symbolic (Alphabetic) Mode

Symbolic mode uses letter codes combined with mathematical operators (+, -, =) to modify specific permission bits without affecting others.

Target Categories (Who):

  • u — User (Owner)
  • g — Group
  • o — Others (World)
  • a — All (u, g, and o)

Operators:

  • + — Add permissions
  • - — Remove permissions
  • = — Set exact permissions (overwriting existing ones)

Permission Types:

  • r — Read
  • w — Write
  • x — Execute

Multiple symbolic actions can be combined by separating them with commas:

darkstar:/tmp/foo# ls -l
total 0
-rw-r--r-- 1 brusso users 0 Nov  2 18:51 a
-rw-r--r-- 1 brusso users 0 Nov  2 18:51 b
-rw-r--r-- 1 brusso users 0 Nov  2 18:51 c
-rw-r--r-- 1 brusso users 0 Nov  2 18:51 d

darkstar:/tmp/foo# chmod u+x a
darkstar:/tmp/foo# chmod g+w b
darkstar:/tmp/foo# chmod u+x,g+x,o-r c
darkstar:/tmp/foo# chmod u=rwx,g=rx,o= d

darkstar:/tmp/foo# ls -l
-rwxr--r-- 1 brusso users 0 Nov  2 18:51 a*
-rw-rw-r-- 1 brusso users 0 Nov  2 18:51 b
-rwxr-x--- 1 brusso users 0 Nov  2 18:51 c*
-rwxr-x--- 1 brusso users 0 Nov  2 18:51 d*

Both Octal and Symbolic modes are widely used. Octal mode is ideal when setting absolute permissions across all three sets at once, while Symbolic mode is perfect for selectively adding or removing a specific privilege.

10.3 SUID, SGID, and the "Sticky" Bit

We're not quite done with permissions just yet. There are three other "special" permissions in addition to those mentioned above: SUID, SGID, and the sticky bit. When a file or directory has one or more of these permissions set, it behaves in special ways. The SUID and SGID permissions change the way an application is executed, while the sticky bit restricts file deletion. These permissions are applied with chmod just like standard read, write, and execute permissions, but with a twist.

When Are Special Permissions Used?

Before diving into how these special bits are configured, it helps to understand the practical scenarios where each permission is necessary:

  • SUID (Set User ID): Used when a regular user needs to run an executable that requires temporary elevated privileges (usually root) to perform a system task. A classic example is changing one's password using /usr/bin/passwd, which must modify system files like /etc/shadow that are strictly owned by root.
  • SGID (Set Group ID): Used on binary executables to run a process with the group owner's permissions, or set on shared directories so that any newly created file automatically inherits the parent directory's group ownership instead of the creator's default group.
  • Sticky Bit: Set on shared directories where all users have full write access (such as /tmp). It restricts file deletion so that users can only remove or rename their own files, preventing them from accidentally or maliciously deleting files owned by others.

SUID and SGID in Action

SUID and SGID stand for "Set User ID" and "Set Group ID", respectively. When an application with one of these bits is set, the process executes with the privileges of the file's owner or group owner, regardless of which user actually launched the program.

Let's look at a common SUID application, /usr/bin/passwd, and the system files it interacts with:

darkstar:~# ls -l /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /usr/bin/passwd
-rw-r--r-- 1 root root     2573 out 21 20:46 /etc/passwd
-rw-r----- 1 root shadow   1408 out 21 20:46 /etc/shadow
-rws--x--x 1 root root   136960 set  2 05:17 /usr/bin/passwd

Notice the permissions on /usr/bin/passwd. Instead of an x in the user's execute slot, there is an s. This indicates that passwd is a SUID executable: when executed by a standard user, the process runs with root privileges. This elevated privilege is required because neither /etc/passwd nor /etc/shadow is writable by unprivileged users.

The sticky bit restricts the ability to delete or rename files and is applied to directories. In a directory with the sticky bit set, non-root users can only delete or rename files that they personally own.

Let's inspect a common system directory that utilizes the sticky bit:

darkstar:~# ls -ld /tmp 
drwxrwxrwt 1458 root root 1269760 nov  2 19:17 /tmp

As a shared directory for temporary file storage, /tmp must be readable, writable, and executable by all users. Setting the sticky bit—indicated by the t in the world execution slot—prevents users from deleting each other's temporary files.

Special Permissions Syntax Reference

Permission Type Octal Value Letter Symbol Affected Slot Primary Function
SUID 4 s User (u) Executes process as file owner
SGID 2 s Group (g) Executes process as group owner / inherits directory group
Sticky Bit 1 t Others (o) Restricts file deletion to file owner

Octal Mode (4 Digits)

When applying special permissions with octal numbers, pass a leading fourth digit to chmod:

darkstar:~# chmod 4711 /usr/bin/passwd
darkstar:~# chmod 1777 /tmp

Note: Whenever the leading fourth octet is omitted, chmod defaults its value to 0.

Symbolic Mode

You can also assign special bits using symbolic notation (s and t):

darkstar:~# chmod u+s,go+x /usr/bin/passwd
darkstar:~# chmod ug+rwx,o+rwt /tmp

11. Working with Filesystems

11.1 The Filesystem Hierarchy

Slackware Linux stores all of its files and directories under a single / directory, typically referred to as "root". This is in stark contrast to what you may be familiar with in Microsoft Windows. Different hard disk partitions, USB flash drives, memory cards (and even CD-ROMs and/or floppy disks, if you still use them) can all be mounted in directories under /, rather than relying on "drive letters" (such as C: or D:).

While these devices can technically be mounted almost anywhere, Slackware provides sensible default locations:

  • The /mnt directory: Its primary purpose is to provide a location for system administrators to mount block devices temporarily. You can mount volumes directly on /mnt (the traditional UNIX approach), use predefined subdirectories such as /mnt/hd or /mnt/tmp, or create custom mount points as needed.

  • The /media directory: Its purpose is to provide a standardized location across different Linux systems for users and desktop applications to mount removable media. For example, desktop environments (such as KDE) or file managers (like Konqueror) can utilize /media for auto-mounting devices. While Slackware traditionally defaults to using /mnt (e.g., /mnt/cdrom as the CD-ROM mount point in /etc/fstab), you can edit /etc/fstab if you prefer to strictly follow FHS (Filesystem Hierarchy Standard) recommendations.

Below is a breakdown of the standard directories found on virtually all Slackware Linux installations and their primary functions:

Directory Description
/ The root directory, under which all others exist
/bin Minimal set of binary programs for all users
/boot The kernel, initrd, and other requirements for booting Slackware
/dev Collection of special files allowing direct access to hardware
/etc System configuration files
/home User directories where personal files and settings are stored
/lib Core shared libraries needed to boot the system and run binaries in /bin and /sbin
/lib64 Essential 64-bit system libraries (on 64-bit Slackware architectures)
/lost+found Directory where fsck stores recovered file fragments after an unclean shutdown
/media Directory for auto-mounting features in DBUS/HAL
/mnt Places to temporarily mount removable media
/opt Directory where some (typically proprietary) software may be installed
/proc Kernel exported filesystem for process information
/root The root user's home directory
/run Volatile runtime data describing system state since the last boot (tmpfs)
/sbin Minimal set of system or superuser binaries
/srv Site-specific data such as web pages served by this system
/sys Special kernel implimentation details
/tmp Directory reserved for temporary files for all users
/usr All non-essential programs, libraries, and shared files
/var Regularly changing data such as log files

11.2 Local Filesystem Types

The Linux kernel supports a wide variety of filesystems, allowing you to choose from a rich set of features tailored to your specific needs. Fortunately, the default filesystem choices in Slackware Linux are well-suited for almost any scenario, from general-purpose desktop computing to high-performance enterprise servers.

The filesystem types listed below are enabled and supported during the Slackware installation process:

11.2.1 ext2

ext2 is the oldest filesystem included in Slackware Linux for storing data on hard disks. Compared to other filesystems, ext2 is simplistic. It is faster than most others for reading and writing data, but does not include any journaling capability. This means that after a hard crash, the filesystem must be exhaustively checked to discover and (hopefully) fix any errors.

ext2 is the oldest filesystem in the ext family included with Linux for storing data on block devices. Compared to modern filesystems, ext2 is simplistic. While it offers fast read and write speeds due to its low metadata overhead, it does not include journaling capabilities. Consequently, after an unclean shutdown or hard system crash, the entire filesystem must undergo an exhaustive consistency check (fsck) to discover and repair errors. Today, ext2 is primarily used for small boot partitions or solid-state flash media where minimizing write cycles is critical.

11.2.2 ext3

ext3 is the direct successor to ext2. It was designed to replace ext2 by adding journaling support while maintaining backwards compatibility with the existing ext2 codebase. Journaling logs pending filesystem changes to a dedicated area before committing them to main storage, drastically reducing recovery times after a system power failure. In fact, ext2 and ext3 share a nearly identical on-disk structure, allowing seamless in-place conversion between the two without data loss. While highly reliable and supported by extensive recovery utilities, ext3 requires periodic, full fsck checks at boot time, which can cause noticeable delays on large volumes.

11.2.3 ext4

ext4 is the default and most widely recommended general-purpose filesystem for Slackware Linux across desktops, laptops, and a lot of enterprise servers. Building upon the foundation of ext3, ext4 introduces substantial performance, capacity, and reliability improvements:

  • Extents: Replaces traditional block mapping with extents, improving large file performance and reducing disk fragmentation.
  • Delayed Allocation: Optimizes block allocation decisions right before writing data to disk, enhancing write speed and storage efficiency.
  • Scalability: Supports volume sizes up to 1 Exabyte (EB) and individual files up to 16 Terabytes (TB).
  • Faster Filesystem Checks: Unallocated block groups are skipped during fsck, making maintenance checks significantly faster than on ext3.

For the vast majority of modern Slackware desktop and server installations, ext4 provides the optimal balance of rock-solid stability and high performance.

11.2.4 JFS

JFS was contributed to the Linux kernel by IBM and is renowned for its low CPU usage and rapid responsiveness even under heavy server workloads. Designed for high-end enterprise systems, JFS handles scaling across massive storage volumes with ease. Its long history of rigorous testing makes it one of the most stable and reliable journaling filesystems available for Linux, particularly for Network-Attached Storage (NAS) appliances.

11.2.5 btrfs
