17.5.3 System Maintenance and Full Upgrades

When upgrading between Slackware releases or tracking the -current development branch, slackpkg provides three essential workflow commands that must be executed in sequence:

  • slackpkg install-new: Scans the remote mirror for newly added packages that do not exist on your local installation and prompts to install them.
  • slackpkg upgrade-all: Upgrades every installed package on your system to match the remote mirror's package tree.
  • slackpkg clean-system: Scans your system for packages that are no longer part of the official Slackware distribution. This identifies obsolete packages removed from the upstream tree as well as custom third-party packages.

Recommended System Upgrade Sequence:

darkstar:~# slackpkg update
darkstar:~# slackpkg install-new
darkstar:~# slackpkg upgrade-all
darkstar:~# slackpkg clean-system

It's a great and easy option to do your system update!

17.5.4 Protecting Packages (/etc/slackpkg/blacklist)

To prevent specific packages—such as custom-compiled kernels, proprietary graphics drivers, or third-party applications—from being accidentally upgraded or removed during automated maintenance, you can add them to the blacklist.

You can blacklist packages by editing /etc/slackpkg/blacklist or by running the interactive blacklist command:

darkstar:~# slackpkg blacklist
Edit /etc/slackpkg/blacklist to add or remove packages.

Packages in blacklist:

mariadb

Blacklisted packages will be excluded from slackpkg upgrade, upgrade-all, and clean-system operations.

18. Keeping Track of Updates

18.1 The -stable Branch

After a new version of Slackware is released, the Slackware development team releases updated packages to fix security vulnerabilities and critical bugs as needed. Therefore, it is essential to stay up to date with patches released for your specific release of Slackware, officially referred to as the -stable branch.

There is also a -current branch, which serves as the active development tree for the next upcoming stable release. Because -current undergoes frequent and potentially disruptive updates, running it requires a willingness to handle system breakage and troubleshoot issues independently. For daily use and production environments, sticking with the -stable branch is strongly recommended.

Since -stable package updates are released after official installation media are produced, they must be downloaded from the Internet. Numerous organizations and universities worldwide maintain public Slackware mirrors containing the full repository tree, like this example: https://mirrors.slackware.com/slackware/slackware-15.0/ (or specifically the patches/ directory).

Updates can be accessed via HTTP/HTTPS, FTP, or synchronized using rsync. To locate an official or geographically close mirror, consult the Slackware mirror list at https://www.slackware.com/getslack/. Choosing a mirror geographically close to your location ensures faster transfer speeds and reduces overall network latency.

Monitoring Updates via ChangeLog.txt

The most reliable way to monitor security patches and package revisions is by inspecting the ChangeLog.txt file maintained at the root of every updated mirror repository.

You can inspect the ChangeLog.txt file directly from your terminal using wget piped into less:

darkstar:~$ wget -O - https://mirrors.slackware.com/slackware/slackware-15.0/ChangeLog.txt | less
Sat Nov  7 23:41:05 UTC 2026
patches/packages/ca-certificates-20261107-noarch-1_slack15.0.txz:  Upgraded.
  This update provides the latest CA certificates to check for the
  authenticity of SSL connections.
+--------------------------+
Fri Nov  6 23:52:17 UTC 2026
patches/packages/httpd-2.4.70-i586-1_slack15.0.txz:  Upgraded.
  This update fixes bugs and security issues.
  For more information, see:
    https://downloads.apache.org/httpd/CHANGES_2.4.70
  (* Security fix *)
... more and more lines ..

18.2 Security Update Mailing List

While the Slackware team occasionally releases updated bugfix-only packages, most system administrators are primarily interested in security advisories for vulnerabilities discovered after a -stable release.

To keep users informed, the Slackware development team maintains an official mailing list that delivers advisories whenever updated packages are issued for critical security issues.

Subscribing to the Security List

To subscribe to the mailing list:

  1. Send an email to <majordomo@slackware.com>.
  2. In the subject line use subscribe slackware-security and place the following single line in the body of the message:
subscribe slackware-security

18.3 Slackware's Roadmap

By default, Slackware does not follow a fixed business roadmap or a rigid release schedule, as target release dates in software development are often pure speculation. It is impossible to predict exactly how much time is required to integrate major upstream package updates, resolve complex dependencies, and thoroughly test system stability.

As new packages and components are prepared for an upcoming release, they are continuously built and uploaded into the -current development tree. If the -current tree is temporarily frozen or unlinked on repository mirrors, it typically indicates that a new stable version of Slackware has just been officially released. A new -current tree is initialized shortly after each stable release to begin the next development cycle.

18.4 Development version

As noted earlier, the active development version of Slackware is publicly accessible within the -current directory across official repository mirrors.

For 64-bit x86_64 architectures, the primary development tree can be browsed at:

  • Slackware 64-bit -current Mirror: https://mirrors.slackware.com/slackware/slackware64-current/

Users and testers who wish to follow ongoing changes, inspect new package additions, or update a testing machine can sync directly against this tree using mirrors, rsync, or package management tools configured for the -current branch.

18.5 Upgrading Slackware Versions

Upgrading from one major release of Slackware to another is considerably more complex than simply updating individual security patches. Each new release introduces widespread changes—and while many are minor, some structural changes can cause severe system breakage if you do not prepare for them properly.

Unlike many Linux distributions that rely on fully automated upgrade scripts, Slackware maintains a hands-on philosophy, putting you in full control of the upgrade process (I love it!).

Step-by-Step Upgrade Guidelines

Before attempting an operating system upgrade, follow these essential guidelines:

  1. Evaluate Necessity: The most important (and frequently overlooked) step is deciding whether an upgrade is actually necessary. If your current installation is stable, secure, and fully meets your operational requirements, upgrading to a newer release may not be needed.

  2. Read CHANGES_AND_HINTS.TXT: If you decide to proceed, carefully read the CHANGES_AND_HINTS.TXT file located on your installation media or mirror repository. This document is actively updated during each development cycle and details key architecture changes, deprecated packages, and workarounds.

  3. Consult UPGRADE.TXT: Thoroughly read UPGRADE.TXT before executing any commands. This file outlines the official, recommended sequence of package upgrades and configuration adjustments required for a successful transition.

  4. Create Full Backups: Always back up critical configuration files and personal data before starting an upgrade. At a minimum, create backups of the /etc (system configurations) and /home (user data) directories. Having complete backups ensures you can restore your system or perform a clean installation if the upgrade encounters critical issues.

Fresh Install vs. In-Place Upgrade

After reviewing CHANGES_AND_HINTS.TXT and UPGRADE.TXT, many system administrators find that backing up user data and performing a fresh installation of the new Slackware release is cleaner, faster, and far less prone to configuration conflicts than attempting a complex in-place upgrade.

Because every new version of Slackware introduces unique changes, static instructions in external guides quickly become outdated. Always rely directly on the official documentation included on your Slackware installation media or repository mirror.

18.6 Practical Upgrade Workflow

After evaluating the benefits and risks of upgrading to a new Slackware version, you can proceed with the upgrade process. While the exact procedures may vary slightly between releases, the general workflow follows the official UPGRADE.TXT guidelines:

  1. Download the new package tree: Download all packages for the target Slackware release from an official mirror (or mount the installation DVD/ISO).
  2. Follow the recommended UPGRADE.TXT sequence:
  3. Switch to single-user mode: Run init 1 to prevent active background daemons and user sessions from interfering with library updates.
  4. Remove obsolete packages: Remove packages that have been deprecated or renamed in the new release using removepkg.
  5. Upgrade glibc shared libraries: Upgrade core system C libraries first (glibc-solibs) so that existing binaries continue to function smoothly during the upgrade process.
  6. Upgrade package management tools: Update pkgtools (including installpkg and upgradepkg) to ensure compatibility with any new package features or compression formats.
  7. Upgrade all remaining packages: Run upgradepkg --install-new to upgrade installed packages and install any new core packages introduced in the release.
  8. Review and update configuration files: Check /etc for new configuration files generated with .new extensions and merge necessary changes into your active configuration.
  9. Update the bootloader: Re-run lilo (or update your ELILO/GRUB configuration) to ensure the bootloader points to the newly installed kernel.
  10. Reboot the system: Restart the machine to load the new kernel and updated system environment.

Warning: Specific upgrade steps can vary depending on the release. Always read the official UPGRADE.TXT file included with your target Slackware version before performing an upgrade.

19. The Linux Kernel

19.1 What Does the Kernel Do?

You have likely heard system administrators and developers talk about compiling or building a custom kernel, but what exactly is the kernel, and what role does it play in Linux?

The kernel is the central component of an operating system. It serves as the fundamental bridge between physical hardware and user applications, acting as the primary software layer responsible for directly managing and orchestrating hardware resources.

+-------------------------------------------------------+
|                 User Applications                     |
|         (Bash Shell, Text Editors, Web Browsers)      |
+-------------------------------------------------------+
                           |
                           v
+-------------------------------------------------------+
|                    Linux Kernel                       |
|   (Process Scheduling, Memory Management, Drivers)    |
+-------------------------------------------------------+
                           |
                           v
+-------------------------------------------------------+
|                 Physical Hardware                     |
|           (CPU, Memory, Disks, Network NICs)          |
+-------------------------------------------------------+

Core Functions of the Kernel

Specifically, the kernel handles low-level hardware interactions and resource management:

  • Hardware Control: It instructs storage drives to read or write data blocks and tells Network Interface Cards (NICs) when to transmit data across physical media.
  • Hardware Interrupt Handling: It continuously listens for events generated by hardware devices. For instance, when a network card receives data packets from a remote computer, it triggers a hardware interrupt, prompting the kernel to receive, process, and pass the data to the appropriate application.
  • Resource Allocation: It manages CPU time, physical RAM usage, and peripheral access across all running processes while enforcing security and isolation boundaries.

Because it operates with complete access to hardware and CPU memory spaces, the kernel is both the single most critical and most complex piece of software on your computer.

19.2 Working with Modules

The complexity of a modern Linux kernel is impressive. Uncompressed, the Linux kernel source code reaches nearly 1.8 GB. With thousands of active developers and hundreds of configurable features, compiling everything statically into a single monolithic binary would result in an impractically large kernel image and unnecessarily high RAM consumption.

To keep the core kernel size compact and RAM usage manageable, most drivers and features are compiled as Loadable Kernel Modules (LKMs). These modules function as dynamic device drivers that can be inserted into or removed from a running kernel on demand without requiring a system reboot. Beyond traditional device drivers, modules also provide support for network protocols, firewall filters, security layers, and filesystems.

Automatic Module Handling with udev

Slackware Linux automatically handles module loading for most hardware devices. During the boot process, the device manager daemon (udevd) probes the system's hardware buses. For every detected component, udevd automatically loads the matching kernel module and creates the corresponding device node under /dev.

Consequently, manual module management is rarely required during routine system operation, though it remains an essential skill when troubleshooting or configuring specialized hardware.

Listing Loaded Modules (lsmod)

To view all kernel modules currently loaded in memory, use the lsmod utility:

darkstar:~# lsmod 
xt_nat                 12288  1
xt_tcpudp              12288  3
veth                   40960  0
xt_conntrack           12288  3
xt_MASQUERADE          12288  4
nf_conntrack_netlink   57344  0
ip6table_nat           12288  1
ip6table_filter        12288  1
ip6_tables             32768  2 ip6table_filter,ip6table_nat
iptable_nat            12288  1
nf_nat                 61440  4 ip6table_nat,xt_nat,iptable_nat,xt_MASQUERADE
nf_conntrack          176128  5 xt_conntrack,nf_nat,xt_nat,nf_conntrack_netlink,xt_MASQUERADE
nf_defrag_ipv6         20480  1 nf_conntrack
nf_defrag_ipv4         12288  1 nf_conntrack
... many more lines ommitted ...

The output displays three columns:

  1. Module: The name of the loaded kernel module.
  2. Size: The memory footprint of the module in bytes.
  3. Used by: The count and names of other loaded modules or processes currently depending on it.

Loading Modules (modprobe vs. insmod)

Linux provides two utilities for inserting modules into the kernel: modprobe and insmod.

  • modprobe (Recommended): Reads dependency mappings from /lib/modules/$(uname -r)/modules.dep. When you request a module by name, modprobe automatically locates and loads any required prerequisite modules first.

  • insmod (Low-level): Inserts a single standalone module file directly. Unlike modprobe, insmod requires the exact filesystem path to the module file (.ko) and does not perform automatic dependency resolution.

darkstar:~# insmod /lib/modules/$(uname -r)/kernel/fs/bfs/bfs.ko
darkstar:~# modprobe cramfs
darkstar:~# lsmod | grep fs
cramfs                 57344  0
mtd                   110592  1 cramfs
bfs                    24576  0

Unloading Modules (modprobe -r vs. rmmod)

Similarly, two programs exist for removing modules from memory: rmmod and modprobe -r.

  • modprobe -r (Recommended): Unloads the specified module and recursively removes any dependent modules that are no longer in use by other processes.
  • rmmod: Unloads only the specified single module. If other active modules depend on it, rmmod will fail and report a busy error.
darkstar:~# rmmod bfs
darkstar:~# modprobe -r cramfs
darkstar:~# lsmod | grep fs
darkstar:~#

19.3 Compiling A Kernel and Why to do So

Most Slackware users will never need to compile a custom kernel. The stock vmlinuz kernel supplied by Slackware includes built-in support and modules for virtually all modern hardware configurations.

However, some users may still need to compile a kernel. If your computer contains bleeding-edge hardware, a newer kernel version may offer improved support. In other cases, a specific kernel patch may be available to correct an issue you are experiencing. Users who simply want the latest kernel version or believe that a custom-compiled kernel will yield better performance can certainly build one, though major performance differences are rarely noticeable on modern systems.

If you decide that compiling a custom kernel is necessary for your environment, this section walks you through each step. Compiling and installing a kernel is straightforward, but several critical mistakes can render your system unbootable.

Obtaining Kernel Source Code

The first step is ensuring the kernel source code is installed on your system. The official kernel source package is included in the k package series in the Slackware installer, or you can download vanilla source archives directly from https://www.kernel.org/. I recommend you use the package.

For the examples in this section, we will use kernel 7.2.14.

Traditionally, the kernel source resides in /usr/src/linux, which is usually a symbolic link pointing to the specific installed kernel release directory. However, this path is not mandatory; you can extract and build kernel source code inside any directory where you have write permissions.

root@darkstar:/usr/src# ls -l
total 16
drwxr-xr-x 3 root root 4096 Oct 13 15:10 bcachefs-1.39.7/
lrwxrwxrwx 1 root root   10 Nov 14 14:17 gmock -> googlemock/
drwxr-xr-x 6 root root 4096 Nov 14 14:17 googlemock/
drwxr-xr-x 7 root root 4096 Nov 14 14:17 googletest/
lrwxrwxrwx 1 root root   10 Nov 14 14:17 gtest -> googletest/
lrwxrwxrwx 1 root root   12 Nov 14 14:15 linux -> linux-7.2.14/
drwxr-xr-x 26 root root 4096 Oct 30 11:49 linux-7.2.14/

Base Kernel Configuration (.config)

The most difficult part of compiling a kernel is the configuration process. With thousands of configurable drivers, subsystems, and options, configuring a kernel from scratch can be overwhelming.

The entire kernel configuration is stored in a hidden file named .config located at the root of the kernel source directory. While .config is a plain-text file, manually editing it with a text editor is strongly discouraged; instead, you should always rely on the kernel's built-in interactive configuration tools.

Unless you are an experienced kernel developer, you should always start with a known, working base configuration and modify it as needed. Building upon an established base prevents you from accidentally omitting critical hardware drivers or filesystem support required to boot your machine.

The best starting points for a solid base .config are the stock configurations provided by Slackware:

  • Installed System: The active configuration is saved directly in /boot/config (or versioned files such as /boot/config-generic-7.2.14).
  • Running Kernel (/proc filesystem): If supported by your active kernel, the exact running configuration can be extracted from /proc/config.gz.
  • Installation Media & Mirrors: Stock configurations can also be found on the Slackware installation media or repository mirrors within the kernels/ directory.

Note: In this example 7.2.14 is a version of kernel that I have Installed in my computer. This number can change.

Setting Up .config from /boot

The most difficult part of compiling a kernel is the configuration process. The entire configuration is stored in a hidden file named .config located at the root of the kernel source directory. While .config is a plain-text file, manually editing it with a text editor is strongly discouraged.

Unless you are an experienced kernel developer, you should always start with a known, working base configuration. The most practical and reliable way is to copy the active kernel configuration file directly from /boot into your kernel source root directory:

darkstar:~# cp /boot/config /usr/src/linux/.config

Note: On systems with multiple versioned configuration files in /boot, copy the specific file matching your active kernel, such as cp /boot/config-generic-7.2.14 /usr/src/linux/.config.

Alternatively, if your running kernel was compiled with /proc/config.gz support, you can extract the active configuration using zcat:

darkstar:~# zcat /proc/config.gz > /usr/src/linux/.config

Customizing Options with make menuconfig

Once your .config base file is in place, you can customize hardware options, filesystem support, and kernel subsystems using the interactive terminal interface:

darkstar:/usr/src/linux# make menuconfig

Running make menuconfig opens an ncurses-driven, menu-based interface. It allows you to navigate submenus using arrow keys, toggle built-in drivers ([*]), loadable modules ([M]), or disabled features ([ ]), and save your updated .config file when finished.

Note: If you are upgrading to a newer upstream kernel version using an existing base configuration, running make oldconfig beforehand will prompt you specifically for any newly introduced kernel features.

Compiling and Installing the Kernel

Once configuration is complete, compile the compressed kernel image (bzImage) and its loadable modules in a single step:

darkstar:/usr/src/linux# make -j$(nproc)

Note: The -j$(nproc) flag tells make to compile using all available CPU cores in parallel, significantly reducing build times.

After compilation completes without errors, install the kernel modules into /lib/modules/ and copy the newly generated kernel binary, symbol map, and configuration file into /boot:

darkstar:/usr/src/linux# make modules_install
darkstar:/usr/src/linux# cp arch/x86/boot/bzImage /boot/vmlinuz-7.2.14-custom
darkstar:/usr/src/linux# cp System.map /boot/System.map-7.2.14-custom
darkstar:/usr/src/linux# cp .config /boot/config-7.2.14-custom

Generating the Initial Ramdisk (initrd)

If your root partition or storage controller drivers (such as NVMe or ext4) are compiled as modules, generate an initial ramdisk image for your custom kernel:

darkstar:~# mkinitrd -c -k 7.2.14 -f ext4 -r /dev/nvme0n1p2 -m nvme:ext4 -o /boot/initrd-custom.gz

Configuring the Bootloader for NVMe Drives

On modern NVMe storage drives, device nodes follow the /dev/nvmeXnY naming scheme (for example, /dev/nvme0n1), with partitions designated by a p suffix (such as /dev/nvme0n1p1 for the EFI System Partition and /dev/nvme0n1p2 for the Linux root / partition).

After compiling the kernel and generating the initrd, update your bootloader configuration to register the new custom kernel while retaining the stock kernel entry as a safe recovery option.

Configuring LILO (/etc/lilo.conf)

If your system uses traditional BIOS/CSM boot with LILO, edit /etc/lilo.conf to append an entry for your custom kernel:

# Global Settings
boot = /dev/nvme0n1
prompt
timeout = 50
vga = normal

# Stock Kernel Entry (Retain for Recovery)
image = /boot/vmlinuz
    root = /dev/nvme0n1p2
    initrd = /boot/initrd.gz
    label = Slack-Stock
    read-only

# Custom Compiled Kernel Entry
image = /boot/vmlinuz-7.2.14-custom
    root = /dev/nvme0n1p2
    initrd = /boot/initrd-custom.gz
    label = Slack-Custom
    read-only

Important: Whenever you modify /etc/lilo.conf, you must execute the lilo command as root to write the changes to the boot sector map:

darkstar:~# lilo

Configuring ELILO (elilo.conf — UEFI Boot)

If your system boots using UEFI firmware, Slackware manages the startup process via ELILO. Because UEFI firmware reads files directly from the VFAT-formatted EFI System Partition (ESP), both the custom kernel binary and its matching initrd-custom.gz image must be copied into the ELILO directory located under /boot/efi/EFI/Slackware/.

  • Step 1: Copy Kernel and initrd Images to the ESP
darkstar:~# cp /boot/vmlinuz-7.2.14-custom /boot/efi/EFI/Slackware/vmlinuz-custom
darkstar:~# cp /boot/initrd-custom.gz /boot/efi/EFI/Slackware/initrd-custom.gz
  • Step 2: Edit /boot/efi/EFI/Slackware/elilo.conf

Note: File paths inside elilo.conf are relative to the directory where elilo.conf resides on the ESP.

darkstar:~# vi /boot/efi/EFI/Slackware/elilo.conf

chooser=simple
delay=1
timeout=1

# Stock Kernel Entry (Retain for Recovery)
image=vmlinuz
    label=vmlinuz
    initrd=initrd.gz
    read-only
    append="root=/dev/sda4 vga=normal ro"

# Custom Compiled Kernel Entry
image=vmlinuz-custom
    label=vmlinuz-custom
    initrd=initrd-custom.gz
    read-only
    append="root=/dev/sda4 vga=normal ro"

Note for ELILO: Unlike LILO, ELILO does not require running an updater binary after saving changes. The UEFI firmware reads elilo.conf directly from the EFI FAT partition during system startup.

After rebooting your system, select your custom kernel from the boot menu, as shown in the image below:

20. Slackware Live Edition

20.1 Introduction to Slackware Live Edition

Slackware Live Edition is an official-equivalent, portable live operating system created and maintained by Eric Hameleers (Alien BOB). Built using the liveslak toolset, Slackware Live Edition runs entirely from a DVD, USB flash drive, or ISO file without modifying local hard drive partitions unless explicitly commanded to do so.

Unlike many derivative distributions that heavily modify the underlying system, alter desktop themes, or strip out core server utilities, Slackware Live Edition remains 100% faithful to stock Slackware. It ships with un-patched stock kernels, standard Slackware package series, and default configuration files, providing an authentic Slackware experience in a zero-installation format.

Primary Goals and Design Philosophy

The liveslak project was designed around several core principles:

  • Showcase Pure Slackware: Provide a pristine, unadulterated Slackware environment for evaluation, demonstration, and educational purposes without hiding kernel boot messages or forcing custom branding.
  • Track the Bleeding Edge (-current): Offer a safe, risk-free environment to test Slackware's development tree (-current) on real hardware without risking productivity loss or system breakage on primary workstation drives.
  • Deterministic, Scripted Builds: Generate ISO images completely from scratch using standard Slackware package mirrors via reproducible Bash scripts.
  • KISS Principle (Keep It Simple, Stupid): Leverage stock Linux kernel features—specifically OverlayFS and SquashFS—without requiring custom-compiled third-party kernel patches or out-of-tree file system drivers.
  • Portability with Persistence: Enable users to carry a fully configured Slackware OS in their pocket on a USB stick, retaining file changes, custom software, and encrypted user home directories across reboots.

Default User Credentials and System State

When booted, Slackware Live Edition configures two standard user accounts:

  • live (Password: live): The default unprivileged user account.
  • root (Password: root): The system administrator account.

By default, the ISO images boot directly into runlevel 4 (graphical desktop mode with display manager login), though users can easily select runlevel 3 (multi-user console mode) or single-user mode at the boot prompt.

20.2 Core Architecture: OverlayFS and SquashFS

Slackware Live Edition achieves its flexibility through a combination of two core Linux kernel technologies: SquashFS (a read-only compressed file system) and OverlayFS (a union mount file system driver integrated into Linux kernel 4.x and newer).

File System Assembly

During ISO creation, Slackware packages are grouped and compressed into individual read-only SquashFS archive modules with the .sxz extension (SquashFS compressed with xz or zstd). When the system boots:

  1. The initial RAM file system (initrd) creates a temporary RAM-based root file system.
  2. The liveslak init script detects the boot media and loop-mounts each .sxz module in a specific numeric sequence.
  3. OverlayFS stacks these read-only layers into a single unified directory tree.
  4. On top of the read-only stack, OverlayFS places a writable upper layer: * Volatile Mode (RAM): For read-only media (DVDs) or unconfigured USBs, writes are held in a RAM disk and discarded upon reboot. * Persistent Mode (USB/Disk): For configured USB drives or container files, write operations are stored permanently on disk.

Module Naming and Prefix Conventions

Modules in /liveslak/system/, /liveslak/addons/, and /liveslak/optional/ follow a strict numeric naming convention (NNNN-modname-version-arch.sxz) that dictates their loading order:

Prefix Range Module Purpose & Contents
0000 Contains the core Slackware /boot directory and kernel structures.
0005 Console OS modules (minimal RAM-based rescue environment).
0010–0019 Modules generated from Slackware series tagfiles (a, ap, d, l, n, x, xap, etc.).
0020–0029 Modules generated from custom package lists (pkglists/*.lst).
0030–0039 Modules generated from local package directories (./local/ or ./local64/).
0099 Live OS configuration module (0099-slackware_zzzconf-*.sxz), containing user setup, hardware tweaks, and system scripts.
0040–0098 Free range reserved for user-created custom addon modules.

20.3 Available ISO Variants (Flavors)

The flagship Slackware Live Edition (based on Slackware -current) is the most comprehensive and feature-complete ISO variant. It delivers a full Slackware system pre-packaged with a rich selection of desktop environments, window managers, and display servers out-of-the-box, allowing you to choose your preferred graphical session directly from the login manager:

  • KDE Plasma: The default, feature-rich desktop environment for modern workstations.
  • XFCE: A fast, highly efficient desktop environment ideal for daily use and lighter hardware.
  • WindowMaker: A classic, ultra-lightweight NeXTSTEP-like window manager for maximum speed.
  • labwc: A lightweight Wayland stacking compositor inspired by Openbox, bringing modern Wayland support out-of-the-box.

In addition to traditional X11 display sessions, modern Slackware Live builds include native Wayland support for compatible desktop environments and compositors.

Official Documentation Note: For specialized builds, historical flavors, or architecture-specific ISO images, consult the official documentation page at https://docs.slackware.com/slackware:liveslak#iso_variants.

20.4 Downloading Slackware Live

Official pre-built ISO images for stable Slackware releases and the -current development branch are hosted across several public mirrors:

  • Primary Repository: https://download.liveslak.org/ (rsync: rsync://liveslak.org/liveslak/)
  • US Mirror: https://us.liveslak.org/ (rsync: rsync://us.liveslak.org/liveslak/)
  • UK Mirror: https://slackware.uk/liveslak/ (rsync: rsync://slackware.uk/liveslak/)
  • Asian Mirror: http://repo.ukdw.ac.id/slackware-live/

Always verify the downloaded ISO file against its provided MD5 checksum prior to writing it to boot media:

darkstar:~$ md5sum -c slackware64-live-current.iso.md5

20.5 Starting Slack Live

After downloading the ISO image and writing it to a bootable USB flash drive or DVD, boot your machine from the live media.

Upon startup, the initial Slackware Live boot menu will appear, as shown below:

If no key is pressed within 30 seconds, the system will automatically boot into the default desktop environment and present the graphical login manager, as shown below:

Once logged in, you have a fully functional Slackware Live system ready for use. The screenshot below shows a desktop session running KDE Plasma:

Notice the Install Slackware icon located directly on the desktop. Double-clicking this shortcut launches the setup2hd utility, allowing you to permanently install Slackware onto your local hard drive or NVMe SSD directly from the live environment.

20.6 Booting and Hardware Compatibility

Slackware Live Edition ISOs are hybrid ISOs, meaning they can be burned directly to optical DVDs or copied verbatim to raw USB block devices using dd or cp:

darkstar:~# dd if=slackware64-live-current.iso of=/dev/sdX bs=1M status=progress; sync

BIOS Booting (Syslinux)

On legacy BIOS systems, Syslinux handles the boot process (isolinux on ISOs, extlinux on ext4 USB partitions). Pressing Tab at the Syslinux boot prompt allows editing kernel parameters directly before pressing Enter to boot.

UEFI Booting (GRUB2)

On modern UEFI systems, GRUB2 presents an interactive boot menu. Pressing the e key on a GRUB menu item opens the command line editor. Press F10 or Ctrl+x to boot with modified parameters, or Esc to discard changes.

UEFI Secure Boot Support

Starting with liveslak-1.5.0, 64-bit Slackware Live ISOs include full support for UEFI Secure Boot. Because Slackware does not officially ship a Microsoft-signed bootloader, liveslak incorporates a signed 3rd-party shim binary (/EFI/BOOT/bootx64.efi, extracted from distribution packages like Fedora, Debian, or openSUSE).

shim validates GRUB and kernel binaries using an Alien BOB public SSL certificate. To boot on a Secure Boot system, you must enroll this certificate into your machine's MOK (Machine Owner Key) database:

  1. Automatic First Boot Enrollment: On first boot, shim will fail validation and launch MokManager (a blue dialog screen). Select Enroll key from disk, navigate to the EFI partition's /EFI/BOOT/ directory, select liveslak.der, confirm enrollment, and reboot.
  2. Pre-enrollment via Linux (mokutil): If running an existing Linux system, pre-import the public key before booting the Live media: text darkstar:~# mokutil --import liveslak.der

21. SlackBuilds.org and Custom Software Building

While official Slackware releases include a comprehensive selection of applications, libraries, desktop environments, and server utilities, users frequently require third-party software not included in the core distribution. On many other Linux distributions, installing third-party software involves adding pre-compiled binary repositories or running automated package helpers.

Slackware takes a community-driven approach. To maintain transparency, system stability, and total administrative control, the Slackware community relies on SlackBuilds.org (SBo)—the official community repository of build scripts.

Instead of hosting pre-compiled binary packages, SlackBuilds.org provides audited, standardized shell scripts (.SlackBuild) and metadata files that automate the compilation of upstream source code into native Slackware packages (.txz).

This chapter covers the inner mechanics of SlackBuilds.org, the structure of build script archives, manual step-by-step compilation workflows, dependency management strategies, automated build helpers like sbopkg, and guidelines for authoring custom SlackBuilds.

21.1 Introduction to SlackBuilds.org

Founded to provide a safe, organized, and standardized repository for third-party software, SlackBuilds.org (SBo) serves as the recognized community repository for Slackware Linux.

Rather than distributing pre-compiled binary files, SlackBuilds.org hosts light, plain-text build projects. Each project contains the instructions necessary to download upstream source code, compile it on your local system, and package the output into a clean, trackable .txz file compatible with Slackware's native pkgtools (installpkg, removepkg, upgradepkg).

Core Design Philosophy

The SlackBuilds.org project adheres strictly to Slackware's core philosophy:

  • No Pre-Compiled Binaries: All software is built from pristine upstream source code directly on the target machine. This ensures binaries are compiled against your system's exact library versions.
  • Full Package Management Integration: Software built via SBo is registered in /var/log/packages/, ensuring clean uninstallation and version tracking.
  • No Unsolicited Modifications: Build scripts avoid patching upstream source code unless necessary to fix compilation errors or adhere to standard Slackware filesystem hierarchy guidelines (/usr, /usr/lib64, /etc).
  • Explicit Administrative Control: Dependency resolution is transparent. While scripts document prerequisites, the administrator retains complete control over what gets compiled and installed.

21.2 Anatomizing a SlackBuild Package Archive

When you download a package project from SlackBuilds.org, you receive a compressed archive (e.g., application.tar.gz). Unpacking this archive reveals a directory containing a standardized set of plain-text files:

File Purpose & Description
appName.SlackBuild The main executable shell script that automates unpacking, configuring, compiling, staging, and archiving the application into a .txz package.
appName.info Plain-text metadata file containing package variables, upstream download URLs, MD5 checksums, maintainer contact info, and direct dependencies.
slack-desc Formatted 11-line package description file required by installpkg and recorded in /var/log/packages/.
README Critical documentation detailing build prerequisites, optional compile-time flags, group/user requirements, and post-installation setup instructions.
Patch/Extra files Optional auxiliary files such as .patch bug fixes, custom .desktop menu shortcuts, or default configuration templates.

21.2.1 The Metadata File (appName.info)

The .info file provides machine-readable metadata used by build helpers and system administrators. Key metadata variables include:

  • PRGNAM: The official program name (must match the script directory name).
  • VERSION: The exact version of the upstream source package.
  • DOWNLOAD / DOWNLOAD_x86_64: Direct HTTP or FTP URLs pointing to the upstream source code tarball.
  • MD5SUM / MD5SUM_x86_64: Cryptographic MD5 checksums used to verify the integrity of the downloaded source code before compilation begins.
  • REQUIRES: A space-separated list of direct prerequisite SlackBuild packages that must be installed on the system before building this script.
  • MAINTAINER & EMAIL: Contact details of the community volunteer responsible for testing and updating the script.

21.2.2 The Build Script (appName.SlackBuild)

The .SlackBuild file is an executable Bourne shell script. When executed by the system administrator, it carries out the following automated build steps:

  1. Environment Initialization: Defines compiler flags (CFLAGS, CXXFLAGS), architecture detection (x86_64), and directory variables (TMP=/tmp/SBo, PKG=/tmp/SBo/package-appName).
  2. Directory Cleanup: Prepares a clean build folder in /tmp/SBo/.
  3. Source Unpacking: Extracts the upstream source archive into the temporary directory and enters the source tree.
  4. Permissions Standardization: Normalizes file ownerships and permissions to safe defaults.
  5. Compilation (configure & make): Runs the build tools (such as ./configure, cmake, or meson) using standard Slackware paths (--prefix=/usr, --libdir=/usr/lib64, --sysconfdir=/etc).
  6. Staging (make install): Installs the compiled binaries, libraries, man pages, and documentation into the temporary staging directory ($PKG).
  7. Optimization & Compression: Strips debugging symbols from binaries/libraries to reduce file size, compresses man pages with gzip, and places documentation files (README, LICENSE, NEWS) in /usr/doc/appName-version/.
  8. Package Creation (makepkg): Copies slack-desc into $PKG/install/slack-desc and executes makepkg to generate the final .txz package in the target directory (defaults to /tmp).

21.2.3 The Description File (slack-desc)

Slackware's package tools require a formatted 11-line description file named slack-desc. Each descriptive line begins with the program name followed by a colon, providing a brief summary of the application's functionality and a link to its homepage.

See a example

# HOW TO EDIT THIS FILE:
# The "handy ruler" below makes it easier to edit a package description.
# Line up the first '|' above the ':' following the base package name, and
# the '|' on the right side marks the last column you can put a character in.
# You must make exactly 11 lines for the formatting to be correct.  It's also
# customary to leave one space after the ':' except on otherwise blank lines.

            |-----handy-ruler------------------------------------------------------|
TriliumNotes: TriliumNotes (organize your thoughts)
TriliumNotes:
TriliumNotes: Trilium is an open-source solution for note-taking and organizing a 
TriliumNotes: personal knowledge base. Use it locally on your desktop, or sync it 
TriliumNotes: with your self-hosted server to keep your notes everywhere you go.
TriliumNotes:
TriliumNotes:
TriliumNotes: Homepage: https://triliumnotes.org/
TriliumNotes:
TriliumNotes:
TriliumNotes:

21.3 Building Software Manually with SlackBuilds

Building software manually using SlackBuilds.org scripts is straightforward and requires no third-party build tools beyond standard Slackware development package series (d/).

Step-by-Step Manual Build Workflow

  1. Locate and Download the Script Archive: Search for the desired package on SlackBuilds.org, selecting your Slackware release version (such as 15.0 or above). Download the script archive (libmd.tar.xz).
  2. Extract the Script Archive: Unpack the archive with tar -xzvf libmd.tar.gz and enter the project folder cd libmd/.
  3. Download Upstream Source Code: Inspect libmd.info to find the exact download URL, then download the source tarball directly into the libmd/ directory.
  4. Inspect README and Prerequisites: Read the README file with cat README to check for required build options or dependencies.
  5. Execute the Build Script: Ensure libmd.SlackBuild is executable and run ./foo.SlackBuild as superuser. Upon successful compilation, the script outputs the location of the generated package in /tmp/.
  6. Install the Generated Package: Install the newly created package using installpkg /tmp/libmd-1.1.0-x86_64-1_SBo.txz.
darkstar:~# tar xzvf libmd.tar.gz 
darkstar:~# cd libmd
darkstar:~# cat libmd.info 
PRGNAM="libmd"
VERSION="1.1.0"
HOMEPAGE="https://www.hadrons.org/software/libmd/"
DOWNLOAD="https://archive.hadrons.org/software/libmd/libmd-1.1.0.tar.xz"
MD5SUM="b2a0650c78d92c7a9632a2a3cdd4f6a6"
DOWNLOAD_x86_64=""
MD5SUM_x86_64=""
REQUIRES=""
MAINTAINER="Matteo Bernardini"
EMAIL="ponce@slackbuilds.org"

darkstar:~# wget https://archive.hadrons.org/software/libmd/libmd-1.1.0.tar.xz
--2026-11-15 21:07:08--  https://archive.hadrons.org/software/libmd/libmd-1.1.0.tar.xz

darkstar:~# installpkg /tmp/libmd-1.1.0-x86_64-1_SBo.tgz

21.4 Managing Dependencies and Custom Build Options

21.4.1 Handling Dependency Chains

Slackware deliberately does not resolve dependencies automatically. When a SlackBuild lists packages in its REQUIRES metadata, you must compile and install those prerequisites first, starting from the deepest dependency up to the main application.

For example, if foo requires libbar, and libbar requires dependency-baz:

  1. Download, build, and install dependency-baz.
  2. Download, build, and install libbar.
  3. Download, build, and install foo.

Building out of order will result in missing header or library errors during compilation.

21.4.2 Passing Environment Variables and Build Flags

Many SlackBuild scripts accept optional environment variables to customize build features (e.g., enabling GTK3, Python support, or specific audio drivers). These optional parameters are documented in the README file.

Pass variables directly on the command line prior to running the script:

darkstar:~/foo# ENABLE_GTK3=yes PYTHON3=yes ./foo.SlackBuild

Customizing Target Output and Tag Suffixes

By default, SBo packages are placed in /tmp with a build tag suffix of _SBo. You can customize these defaults using environment variables:

  • OUTPUT: Sets an alternate output directory (e.g., OUTPUT=/usr/packages).
  • TAG: Sets a custom build tag suffix (e.g., TAG=_custom).
darkstar:~/foo# OUTPUT=/usr/packages TAG=_custom ./foo.SlackBuild

21.5 Automated Build Helpers: sbopkg

While manual compilation offers complete transparency, managing multiple third-party packages and complex dependency chains manually can become labor-intensive.

sbopkg is the primary community-developed, terminal-based package helper for SlackBuilds.org. It automates synchronizing the SBo repository tree, downloading source tarballs, building queue files, and installing finished packages.

21.5.1 Installing and Synchronizing sbopkg

Install sbopkg using installpkg, then synchronize its local index with the remote SlackBuilds.org repository:

darkstar:~# wget https://github.com/sbopkg/sbopkg/releases/download/0.38.3/sbopkg-0.38.3-noarch-1_wsr.tgz
darkstar:~# installpkg sbopkg-0.38.3-noarch-1_ws.txz
darkstar:~# sbopkg -r

Note: To check the latest version about sbopkg visit: https://www.sbopkg.org/index.php

Running sbopkg -r clones or updates the SBo tree into /var/lib/sbopkg/SBo/15.0/.

21.5.2 Essential sbopkg Commands

sbopkg can be run interactively via an ncurses menu or directly from the command line:

  • Search for a package (-s): sbopkg -s foo
  • Inspect package info and README (-i): sbopkg -i foo
  • Build and install a package (-b): sbopkg -b foo
  • Process a dependency queue file (-k): sbopkg -k foo.sqf

Note: Even when using sbopkg, always review the README files for queued packages to check for required group/user additions or post-install steps.

darkstar:~#  sbopkg -b awstats

       dom 15 nov 2026 21:14:55 -03

21.6 Authoring and Submitting Custom SlackBuilds

For system administrators and package maintainers who wish to submit new scripts to SlackBuilds.org or maintain local custom software, SBo enforces strict packaging guidelines to maintain repository quality.

21.6.1 Submission Guidelines Checklist

Submissions to SlackBuilds.org must meet the following technical criteria:

  1. Pristine Upstream Archives: Build scripts must fetch untouched upstream source tarballs (.tar.gz, .tar.xz, .tgz). Distributing repackaged or modified binaries is prohibited.
  2. Architecture Flexibility: Scripts must support 32-bit (i586/i686) and 64-bit (x86_64) architectures using standard library suffix detection (LIBDIRSUFFIX="64").
  3. Clean Staging (PKG): Software must be staged inside $PKG (/tmp/SBo/package-PRGNAM) during make install and never directly onto the host root filesystem.
  4. Standard Documentation Path: Documentation must be installed under /usr/doc/$PRGNAM-$VERSION/.
  5. Relative Symbolic Links: Symlinks inside $PKG must be relative links, or generated dynamically in install/doinst.sh via makepkg -l y -c n.
  6. Valid Cryptographic Hashes: Every source file listed in .info must include an accurate MD5 checksum.

By leveraging SlackBuilds.org and mastering custom build script techniques, Slackware system administrators can expand their software library while maintaining a clean, fully logged, and transparent operating system.

22. Slackware ARM

Slackware ARM is the official port of Slackware Linux for 32-bit (ARM) and 64-bit (AArch64) architectures. The project was created, developed, and continues to be maintained by Stuart Winter (MoZes@slackware), Slackware's ARM Platform Architect and Developer. Brent Earl, a member of the Slackware Core Team, assists with research, development, testing, and hardware support across various device models.

22.1 Project Goals and Philosophy

The Slackware ARM project aligns closely with the core UNIX design principles of Slackware while adapting to the unique characteristics of the ARM ecosystem.

Personal and Educational Goals

  • Engaging Technical Challenge: Serves as a hands-on technical project that keeps developers and users intimately connected with UNIX-style operating system internals.
  • Community Collaboration: Fosters a collaborative environment for like-minded developers and enthusiasts to maintain an architecture-specific port.
  • Educational Outreach: Functions as an educational tool for learning low-level system design, supported through official documentation and community media channels.

22.2 Technical Design Goals

Because the ARM hardware landscape differs significantly from traditional x86 systems, Slackware ARM establishes clear technical objectives:

  • Consistent User Experience: Delivers an operating system environment and administrative workflow that mirrors the traditional x86/x86_64 Slackware experience as closely as possible.
  • Standard Slackware Installer: Retains the familiar setup-based text interface for installation.
  • Mainline Linux Kernel: Relies on official mainline kernel sources with minimal downstream patching to ensure long-term stability and upstream compatibility.
  • Selective Package Management: Includes only additional packages necessary for platform-specific hardware support, SoC initialization, or native operating system compilation.
  • Intentional Package Omissions: Excludes x86-only software (such as legacy x86 bootloaders or graphics drivers for hardware non-existent on ARM) and packages that cannot be built for the architecture.
  • Standards-Based Booting: Follows community and industry boot standards (such as U-Boot and UEFI/EDK2) across supported hardware platforms.

22.3 Installation Overview

Unlike the standardized x86 architecture where a single installation image boots almost any machine via standard BIOS or UEFI, ARM devices rely on diverse System-on-Chip (SoC) designs, custom firmware implementations, and Device Tree descriptions.

Consequently, while the installation process utilizes the familiar Slackware installer interface, the initial boot setup and storage preparation depend on your specific hardware model.

Official Documentation & Board Support: Before attempting an installation, consult the official Slackware ARM installation guide for device-specific instructions, firmware requirements, and supported hardware models: https://docs.slackware.com/slackwarearm:inst

23. Slackware in Docker

Containerization has revolutionized software development, deployment, and testing. While Slackware is traditionally known for bare-metal servers and workstations, running Slackware inside Docker containers provides a lightweight, reproducible, and isolated environment for testing configurations, running isolated services, and building software packages.

Official and community-maintained container images for Slackware are available via the community registry at registry.slackware.nl. These images support both 15.0 (stable) and -current (development) releases across x86_64 and i586 architectures.

Note: The release tags and supported architectures described in this chapter reflect the repository status as of November 2026 (Slackware 15.0 and -current). As future stable versions—such as Slackware 16.0—are released, new image tags will be made available. Always consult registry.slackware.nl for the most up-to-date list of available tags.

23.1 Image Architecture: Base vs. Builder

Slackware container images are divided into two primary tiers:

  1. Base Images (slackware:15.0, slackware:current): A minimal, stripped-down Slackware installation containing essential system utilities and a configured slackpkg package manager. Base images are designed to keep container footprints small for production services and general use.

  2. Builder Images (slackware-builder:15.0, slackware-builder:current): A heavy-duty development environment equipped with the full Slackware toolchain—including gcc, g++, make, cmake, meson, ninja, llvm, rust, go, python3-build, and kernel-headers. These images allow developers to compile software and execute SlackBuild scripts immediately without downloading build tools at runtime.

23.2 Quick Start: Running Slackware Containers

  1. Launching an Interactive Session

To pull and start an interactive bash session in a clean Slackware 15.0 container:

darkstar:~\$ docker run -it --rm registry.slackware.nl/slackware/slackware:15.0 /bin/bash
Unable to find image 'registry.slackware.nl/slackware/slackware:15.0' locally

23.3 Building Packages with slackware-builder

Compiling software directly on a host machine can contaminate the host system with temporary header files or build dependencies. Using the slackware-builder image ensures clean, isolated build environments.

Workflow: Executing a SlackBuild in Docker

Suppose you have a local directory (~/builds/htop) containing htop.SlackBuild, htop.info, and the source tarball. You can mount your local directory into the container using a Docker volume (-v), execute the build, and output the compiled .txz package back to your host filesystem:

darkstar:~\$ docker run --rm \
  -v ~/builds/htop:/build \
  -w /build \
  registry.slackware.nl/slackware/slackware-builder:current \
  bash -c "sh htop.SlackBuild && cp /tmp/htop-*.txz /build/"

When the command completes: 1. The package is built inside the isolated container using the pre-installed compiler toolchain. 2. The finished .txz package is saved in your local ~/builds/htop directory on the host. 3. The container self-destructs (--rm), leaving your host system untouched.

23.4 Automated Custom Builder Images (build-builder-image.sh)

For system administrators and developers maintaining custom build environments or private container registries, Eric Hameleers Alien BOB maintains the official container build scripts and Dockerfiles on the Slackware Community Forge:

  • Repository Location: https://forge.slackware.nl/slackware/docker-images

Obtaining the Build Scripts

Before generating custom images, clone the repository to your host system using git:

darkstar:~\$ git clone https://forge.slackware.nl/slackware/docker-images.git
darkstar:~\$ cd docker-images/scripts

This repository contains the build-builder-image.sh script alongside the full set of Dockerfile templates and configuration files required to build both base and development images.

How Image Verification Works

To prevent unnecessary rebuilds, the build script uses a smart base digest check: 1. It inspects the remote base image's unique SHA256 digest (slackware.builder.base-digest). 2. It checks if a local builder image already exists with a matching digest label. 3. If the base image has been updated on the registry, the script automatically triggers a rebuild of the builder image; otherwise, it skips the build process.

Command-Line Usage

The build script supports arguments for targeting specific releases and architectures:

# Build the builder image for Slackware 15.0 (x86_64)
darkstar:~# ./build-builder-image.sh --version current --arch x86_64
brusso@maria ~/t/d/scripts (master)> ./build-builder-image.sh --version current --arch x86_64

# Force a rebuild even if the base image digest has not changed
darkstar:~# ./build-builder-image.sh --version current --arch x86_64 --force

Script Flags Reference

Flag Description Example
-v, --version <ver> Specifies the target Slackware version (15.0 or current). Default is current. ./build-builder-image.sh -v 15.0
-a, --arch <arch> Specifies target architecture (x86_64 or i586). Default is x86_64. ./build-builder-image.sh -a x86_64
-f, --force Forces image creation even if the base image digest remains unchanged. ./build-builder-image.sh --force

23.5 Best Practices for Slackware Dockerfiles

When writing custom Dockerfile scripts based on Slackware, follow these guidelines:

  1. Chain Commands in a Single RUN Block: Combine package updates, software installation, and cache cleanup into a single layer to minimize the final image footprint.
FROM registry.slackware.nl/slackware/slackware:current

RUN slackpkg -batch=on update && \
    slackpkg -batch=on install python3 libffi && \
    slackpkg -batch=on clean && \
    rm -rf /var/cache/slackpkg/* /tmp/*
  1. Set Non-Interactive Environment Variables: Disable interactive prompts during script execution.
  2. Use Specific Image Tags: Avoid using :latest in production Dockerfiles; pin your base images to specific tag such as :15.0 or :current.

Building and Verifying Custom Docker Images

  1. Building the Container Image

To compile your custom image using the local Dockerfile, run docker build:

darkstar:~# docker build -t slack:1 .
[+] Building 30.4s (6/6) FINISHED                                                                                                                                                                                            docker:default
 => [internal] load build definition from Dockerfile                                                                                                                                                                                   0.0s
 => => transferring dockerfile: 255B                                                                                                                                                                                                   0.0s
 => [internal] load metadata for registry.slackware.nl/slackware/slackware:current                                                                                                                                                     0.0s

24. Configuring and Enabling Core Server Services

One of Slackware's greatest strengths as a server operating system is its simplicity and predictability. Unlike modern Linux distributions that rely on complex initialization daemons and unit configuration targets, Slackware adheres strictly to a traditional BSD-style init system.

In Slackware, server services and network daemons are managed via standalone shell scripts stored in /etc/rc.d/. All server software provided by default—such as web servers, database engines, mail transfer agents, and file shares—is installed out-of-the-box in a disabled state for safety.

24.1 The Slackware Service Management Philosophy

To enable or disable a server service in Slackware, you simply change the execute permissions of its corresponding init script in /etc/rc.d/.

  1. Enabling a Service for Automatic Boot Startup To configure a service to start automatically whenever the system boots, grant execution permissions (+x) to its script:
darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.<service>
  1. Disabling a Service To prevent a service from launching at boot time, remove execution permissions (-x):
darkstar:~# chmod -x /etc/rc.d/rc.<service>
  1. Controlling Running Services Manually Regardless of whether a service is enabled at boot, you can manually start, stop, restart, or check its status as root:
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.<service> start
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.<service> stop
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.<service> restart
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.<service> status

24.2 Core Server Services Available in Slackware

Below is a categorized breakdown of the primary server-grade daemons included in the default Slackware software repositories (from the n/, ap/, and a/ package sets).

Note: The goal of this section is not to provide an in-depth technical explanation of how each underlying protocol or service functions, but rather to show you how to enable, configure, and manage these daemons within a Slackware environment.

24.2.1. Web and Database Stack (LAMP)

Apache HTTP Server (httpd)

  • Package: httpd (Apache HTTP Server)
  • Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.httpd
  • Configuration: /etc/httpd/httpd.conf
  • Description: The industry-standard Apache web server. Out-of-the-box, it includes support for SSL/TLS, Virtual Hosts, and module integration (such as PHP).
  • Official Web site: https://httpd.apache.org/

Enabling and starting Apache:

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.httpd
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.httpd start

MariaDB Database Server (mariadb)

  • Package: mariadb (Drop-in replacement for MySQL Database Server)
  • Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.mysqld
  • Configuration: /etc/my.cnf and /etc/my.cnf.d/
  • Description: Enterprise open-source relational database server.
  • Official Web site: https://mariadb.org/

Initial Setup & Activation: Before launching MariaDB for the first time, initialize the system database files:

darkstar:~# mariadb-install-db --user=mysql
darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.mysqld
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.mysqld start
darkstar:~# mariadb-secure-installation

PHP Scripting Language Engine (php) * Package: php is a general-purpose scripting language geared toward web development. * Configuration: /etc/httpd/mod_php.conf and /etc/php.ini * Description: Enables PHP execution within the Apache web server. * Activation: Uncomment the PHP module loader line inside /etc/httpd/httpd.conf:

apache Include /etc/httpd/mod_php.conf

  • Official Web site: https://www.php.net/

24.2.2. Secure Remote Access and Management

OpenSSH Daemon (openssh) * Package: openssh is the premier connectivity tool for remote login with the SSH protocol. * Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.sshd * Configuration: /etc/ssh/sshd_config * Description: Encrypted remote shell access and secure file transfer (SFTP/SCP). * Official Web site: https://www.openssh.org/

Enabling OpenSSH:

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.sshd
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.sshd start

24.2.3. File Sharing and Network Storage

Samba File and Print Server (samba) * Package: samba is the most feature-rich Open Source implementation of the SMB and Active Directory protocols for Linux and UNIX-like systems. * Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.samba * Configuration: /etc/samba/smb.conf * Description: Provides SMB/CIFS file and print sharing for Windows, macOS, and Linux clients, as well as Active Directory integration. * Official Web site: https://www.samba.org/

Enabling Samba:

darkstar:~# cp /etc/samba/smb.conf-sample /etc/samba/smb.conf
darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.samba
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.samba start

24.2.4 Network File System (nfs-utils)

  • Package: nfs-utils is a protocol allowing remote access to a filesystem through the network
  • Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.nfsd
  • Configuration: /etc/exports
  • Description: High-performance native UNIX/Linux network file sharing.
  • Official Web site: https://linux-nfs.org/

Enabling NFS Server:

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.nfsd
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.nfsd start

24.2.5 FTP Daemons (vsftpd / proftpd)

  • Packages: vsftpd (Very Secure FTP Daemon) / proftpd (ftp server written for use on Unix and Unix-a-like operating systems)
  • Init Scripts: /etc/rc.d/rc.inetd / /etc/rc.d/rc.inetd
  • Description: Secure, high-speed FTP server implementations.
  • Official Web site: https://security.appspot.com/vsftpd.html and http://www.proftpd.org/

To enable vsftpd

darkstar:~# vi /etc/inetd.conf

Uncomment the line, removing the symbol #

ftp     stream  tcp     nowait  root    /usr/sbin/tcpd  vsftpd

To enable proftpd

darkstar:~# vi /etc/inetd.conf

Uncomment the line, removing the symbol #

ftp     stream  tcp     nowait  root    /usr/sbin/tcpd  proftpd

And restart the service inetd

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.inetd
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.inetd restart

24.2.6 Domain Name System (DNS) and DHCP

BIND DNS Server (bind) * Package: bind is a DNS server * Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.bind * Configuration: /etc/named.conf * Description: The Internet standard Domain Name System (named) daemon for authoritative and caching name servers. * Official Web site: https://www.isc.org/bind/

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.bind
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.bind start

Dnsmasq DNS/DHCP Server (dnsmasq) * Package: dnsmasq provides network infrastructure for networks * Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.dnsmasq * Configuration: /etc/dnsmasq.conf * Description: A lightweight DNS forwarder, DHCP server, and router advertisement daemon ideal for small business networks or home routers. * Official Web site: https://thekelleys.org.uk/dnsmasq/doc.html

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.dnsmasq
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.dnsmasq start

24.2.7. Mail Services (MTA / IMAP / POP3)

Postfix Mail Transfer Agent (postfix) * Package: postfix is mail server and as an alternative to the widely-used Sendmail program. * Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.postfix * Configuration: /etc/postfix/main.cf * Description: High-performance, secure SMTP mail transfer agent. Slackware also includes classic sendmail as an alternative. * Official Web site: https://www.postfix.org/

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.postfix
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.postfix start

Dovecot IMAP/POP3 Server (dovecot) * Package: dovecot is an open source email server for Linux/UNIX-like systems * Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.dovecot * Configuration: /etc/dovecot/dovecot.conf * Description: Secure, enterprise-grade IMAP and POP3 server for mail client access. * Official Web site: https://dovecot.org/

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.dovecot
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.dovecot start

24.2.8. Network Infrastructure and Security

Network Time Protocol (ntp) * Package: ntp is a protocol designed to synchronize the clocks of computers over a network to a common timebase * Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.ntpd * Configuration: /etc/ntp.conf * Description: Keeps system clock precisely synchronized across the network. * Official Web site: https://www.ntp.org/

darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.ntpd
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.ntpd start

24.2.9 OpenVPN Server (openvpn)

  • Package: openvpn is a full-featured open source SSL VPN solution
  • Init Script: /etc/rc.d/rc.openvpn
  • Configuration: /etc/openvpn/
  • Description: SSL/TLS Virtual Private Network server for secure site-to-site or road-warrior access.
  • Official Web site: https://community.openvpn.net/
darkstar:~# chmod +x /etc/rc.d/rc.openvpn
darkstar:~# /etc/rc.d/rc.openvpn start

24.3 Server Services Reference Summary

Service Category Daemon / Software Init Script Path Primary Config File
Web Server Apache (httpd) /etc/rc.d/rc.httpd /etc/httpd/httpd.conf
Database MariaDB (mariadb) /etc/rc.d/rc.mysqld /etc/my.cnf
Remote Access OpenSSH (sshd) /etc/rc.d/rc.sshd /etc/ssh/sshd_config
File Sharing (SMB) Samba (smbd/nmbd) /etc/rc.d/rc.samba /etc/samba/smb.conf
File Sharing (NFS) NFS Utilities /etc/rc.d/rc.nfsd /etc/exports
FTP vsftpd /etc/rc.d/rc.vsftpd /etc/vsftpd.conf
DNS Server BIND (named) /etc/rc.d/rc.bind /etc/named.conf
DNS / DHCP Dnsmasq /etc/rc.d/rc.dnsmasq /etc/dnsmasq.conf
Mail Transfer Postfix /etc/rc.d/rc.postfix /etc/postfix/main.cf
Mail Delivery Dovecot /etc/rc.d/rc.dovecot /etc/dovecot/dovecot.conf
Time Sync NTP (ntpd) /etc/rc.d/rc.ntpd /etc/ntp.conf
VPN OpenVPN /etc/rc.d/rc.openvpn /etc/openvpn/*.conf
